0 Maintaining soft-landing call amid US labor market slowdown…

…but shifting risks support cutting cycles (Fed to start with -50bp)

0 Inflation moves prompt policy action in Asia (BoJ hike, cuts elsewhere)

• Up next: US Aug Core CPI (0.2%), ECB (-25bp)

## Goldilocks with wrinkles

The news this week is clear in its message: The US expansion continues to downshift while the global expansion remains on track but imbalanced. What this means for the outlook is less clear. We are inclined to view the latest data as consistent with our soft-landing call that sees sufficient supports to keep the expansion aloft, as inflation’s return to target green-lights an easing cycle that aligns rates with mid-cycle fundamentals. In the event, global and US growth are moderating now but will pick up as rate cuts gain traction in 1H25. Unfortunately, soft landings are as hard to track as they are to forecast, as a deceleration to a softlanding looks painfully similar to a slide into recession. Navigating this path requires a risk management approach, which is underscored by the ongoing shifts in the risk balance assessments made by central banks in their forward guidance.

The US is leading the global expansion, both in terms of its robust recovery as well as the latest loss of labor market momentum. Today’s reported 118k gain in private employment is disappointing. The anticipated bounce-back from perceived July distortions did not happen. Rather, a 62k downward prevision to the prior two months reinforces the signal of a slowdown. The %6m run-rate of private employment ticked down to 1.2%ar last month and is nearing the 1% threshold typically seen around recessions (Figure 1; 1.1% given the upcoming benchmark revisions). Although the unemployment rate edged down to 4.2%, the move was just 3bp unrounded and the real-time Sahm rule actually moved up further from 0.53 to 0.57 (now 7bp above the 0.5% recession threshold).

Notwithstanding recession rules, we see resilience as the baseline given limited evidence of a broader break in behavior. A 142k gain in overall US payrolls is

hardly a weak reading, while unemployment insurance claims have trended back down in recent weeks. At the same time, consumer spending continues to advance at a healthy clip, supported by strong fundamentals. Indeed, labor income gains have slowed but remain solid while the recent 15% tumble in oil prices – which owes to expectation of excess OPEC supply – will be a material boost to household purchasing power in the coming quarter (Figure 2).

Beyond the US, the global expansion continues even if on an imbalanced path. Manufacturing remains subdued, with the August output PMI dipping below 50 for the first time since late last year and the forward-looking new orders index pointing to ongoing malaise. However, activity in the larger service sector is strengthening, with the output PMI ticking higher in August and adding to the up-trend that began late last year. Combined, the composite PMI ticked higher and is consistent with a strong 3.1%ar pace of global GDP growth, over a %-point stronger than our current quarter outlook (Figure 3). As the US downshifts from prior strength, growth in Europe and China needs to play a larger role supporting the global expansion. For now, the data do not show much lift, with Western Europe and China tracking a modest 1%ar and 3%ar pace of growth this quarter, respectively.

![](images/cf9a214e120106ab598817e0a21d5491e1a96d6ea150aed178809435f3d92276.jpg)

On balance, our Goldilocks soft-landing call remains premised on the belief that recessions require the interaction of tightening financial conditions with macroeconomic imbalances – and are usually amplified by adverse shocks. The past two years have seen tight borrowing costs along with a range of shocks (supply chains, Russia invasion, gas price surge, US small bank stress). However, absent imbalances from excess leverage or extended credit and investment cycles, it is hard to see the catalyst for recession.

## DM central bank easing ramping up

With inflation on a sustainable path to target and downside growth risks edging up, the time for central bank easing is now. For the Fed, the balance of risks around its dual mandate has shifted considerably in just three months. In June, Powell noted that risks around inflation and labor markets were shifting “into better balance.” At the July meeting, the risks appeared more balanced, with the committee “attentive to both sides of its dual mandate.” By Jackson Hole, Powell

seemed to nudge the needle a little further with a focus on labor market conditions. Today’s payroll report shifted the bias fully, as Governor Waller stated explicitly this morning that, at least for him, “I believe that the balance of risks is now weighted more toward downside risks to the FOMC’s maximum-employment mandate.” Following the July payroll report, we expected 125bp of cuts by year-end. While it is a close call, we maintain our forecast for a front-loaded 50bp cut at the September 17-18 meeting.

Beyond the Fed, other DM CBs are already on the move. The Bank of Canada cut 25bp again this week, bringing the policy rate down to 4.25%. This third consecutive cut puts Canada in the lead of the DM easing cycle so far. Up next week will be the ECB, which is almost certain to cut rates 25bp and signal a quarterly pace of easing. The staff forecast for near-term core inflation is likely to be revised a touch higher due to recent upside surprises, while growth is likely to be nudged lower over 2025-26. This balance underscores the increasing concerns that adverse supply shocks hitting the Euro area (mainly Germany) could be more persistent.

## Japan: A virtuous cycle kicks in

Japan’s higher inflation has started to translate into higher wage growth. This week’s July wage data showed a trend rise in base wages to 3%oya in a fixed sample base, reflecting the gradual implementation of the 3.6% base wage increase from the 2024 spring wage negotiations. We expect this upward trend in wages to continue for the time being, keeping wage growth high into next year. Amid rising inflation expectations, this week's MoF Corporate Survey also showed a strong gain in corporate earnings in 2Q, which will likely be a major support for wage increases heading into next year (Figure 4). After more than two years of stagnation in real wages until 1H24, demand for wage increases is high. While underlying inflation has been moderating since the start of the year, we expect this trend to reverse in response to strong wage growth. With confirmation of price and wage inflation, the BoJ is likely to deliver an additional rate hike in December.

## China policy pivot under way

The August PMIs underscored the ongoing weakness in China’s domestic demand even as export activity looks to be

perking up (we expect August exports to rise 5.5%oya, or 1.6% m/m). Another quarter of below-trend growth (we expect 3%ar in 3Q) is likely to prompt a step up in policy supports. Government bond issuance has accelerated, and special local government bonds are being used to support consumption and government purchases of housing. Consumer spending could further benefit from a proposed 75bp cut in borrowing rates on outstanding mortgages, although the impact of such a move is likely to be modest. More important are growing signals of a policy pivot toward supporting consumption and service activity, including an early relaxation on educational tutorial policies and a more constructive stance on the gaming industry.

## Green light for EM Asia rate cuts

August CPI data confirmed benign inflation trends across EM Asia. In Indonesia, the Philippines, and Thailand, August core inflation was soft, and slowing food and/or energy inflation is helping to bring headline inflation down. Thanks to significant food disinflation, Indonesia’s CPI could slip below target by the end of the year. Despite a weaker and delayed passthrough from rice tariff cuts, Philippine inflation remains firmly within target. Thailand’s CPI is also set to return to the lower bound of the 1-3% target by year-end. All of this reinforces our call for monetary policy easing to start in coming months, with the Philippines already having cut rates in August. Korea and Taiwan face a more complicated trade-off. In Korea, a return to the 2% inflation target supports a rate cut, but financial stability concerns continue to linger amid a re-acceleration in household credit growth. Meanwhile, the strength of Taiwan’s economy – with output gaps closed and strong external demand flowing into domestic demand – are expected to keep the CBC on hold for the foreseeable future. The domestic property sector boom may also trigger an additional tightening in lending conditions, and this week’s August CPI print came in modestly above expectations at 2.4%oya.

## Asian manufacturing resilience

Even as global manufacturing concerns have risen, Asia remains resilient. The Asian manufacturing PMI (excluding China and India) ticked higher and remains close to two-year highs. Encouragingly, both the output and orders index moved up, and the orders to inventory ratio recouped some of its July decline. Much of this resilience stems from Japan, Taiwan, Korea, and Singapore, which have all benefitted from strong global tech demand. However, the drop in the new export orders PMI in August is somewhat concerning, and the sharp contraction in Korea’s July IP poses downside risks to the strong GDP rebound we have penciled in for 3Q. That being said, August customs exports turned higher with gains in both tech and non-tech exports (notably autos). Taiwan’s

manufacturing orders-to-inventory ratio has retraced recently, reflecting an outperformance in IP relative to exports, but the upcoming launch of new smartphone models should help boost exports.

## France: still risk of modest fiscal slippage

President Macron this week nominated Michel Barnier as Prime Minister. Barnier has been part of the right-wing Republicans party and was also chief Brexit negotiator for the EU. Following this announcement, the left NFP coalition indicated it would trigger a no-confidence motion. However, the National Rally appears unlikely to support the motion, which means the Barnier government will likely survive the vote. The government is expected to present a 2025 budget on October 1. Meeting the official 2024 deficit forecast of 5.1% will require savings of 0.5% of GDP. Barnier is a fiscal conservative who will want to comply with the deficit reduction objective, but there is a risk of some slippage.

## Türkiye: Fiscal tightening to aid disinflation

August inflation fell sharply to 52%oya, although monthly price gains firmed to 2.5% (2.9% for core) amid lira depreciation. A tightening fiscal stance is likely to promote disinflation ahead, and we see downside risks to our inflation forecasts of 42.5%oya for end-2024 and 25% for end-2025. The medium-term economic program announced this week revised down this year’s central government deficit target to 4.9% of GDP (previously 6.4%) with a further narrowing to 3.1% seen in 2025 (previously 3.4%). Last quarter’s GDP growth slowed sharply to 0.3%ar from 5.6% in 1Q24, and we continue to look for GDP contractions in both 3Q and 4Q. We still expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate by 250bp each in November and December to 45% and to 30% by end-2025. The CBRT could have more space to cut next year if the fiscal tightening projected in the medium-term economic program is delivered.

## Mexico: Stuck in low gear

Amid deepening domestic and regional uncertainties, growth in Mexico is stuck in low gear. Better data on manufacturing offers hope of a long-awaited recovery in external demand, but domestic demand supports look lackluster. A softening in US job creation also limits upside risks. Investment is expected to moderate significantly amid lingering institutional uncertainties and the post-election spending cuts required to meet fiscal targets. The political transition in the US and the incoming review of USMCA are increasingly in focus and threaten to deepen the rift between Mexico and its diplomatic counterparts. Despite the peso’s slide, we expect a rate cut from Banxico this month, and growth disappointments and Fed easing should pave the way for further cuts from December onward.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 3Q24 global real GDP growth edged down this week to 2.0%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster meanwhile ticked up to 2.1%ar, still suggesting balanced risks to global growth.

About 30% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 3Q growth in around 35% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model estimate for 3Q improved this week. It is now tracking a moderation in global capex growth to 1.1%ar after 3.0%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

Strength in the service PMI lifted the global all-industry index 0.3-pt to 52.8 in August, consistent with global growth of 3.1%ar. But the manufacturing output PMI continued to struggle, falling to 49.9, and our global manufacturing expectations index took a step back last month.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds have rallied this week amid concerns over weakness in US labor market prints. In the US, the somewhat softer tone overall in the labor market report along with dovish Fedspeak, particularly from Fed Governor Waller, means we should continue to see the Fed cutting by 50bps at both the Sep and Nov meetings. With markets pricing in only modestly less easing by year-end we remain more cautious on outright shorts given the negative carry, we continue to prefer steepeners as a bullish proxy and combine the tactical 3s/5s steepeners added earlier this week with our long-standing 5s/30s steepeners to a 3s/30s steepener.

In the Euro area, the ECB looks almost certain to cut rates by 25bp at the Sep meeting, and we see it implicitly messaging a quarterly pace of cuts. With valuations in line with our medium-term targets, we stay neutral on duration outright, hold 2Yx2Y/15Yx15Y EUR swap curve steepeners as a bullish proxy, and stay neutral on France-Germany. In the UK, the cumulative profile of easing by mid-2025 looks modestly rich vs. our expectation of a more patient cycle, but we do not look to fade this given hard landing risks and keep 5s/30s SONIA steepeners as a bullish proxy. In Japan, we see valuations at the richer end of the spectrum and hold 2s/10s JGB curve steepeners.

In EM, the broader market remains sensitive to US developments given the uncertainty over softer vs. harder landing scenarios. We stay neutral overall on EM local bond duration, with OWs in India, Czechia, Türkiye and Uruguay offset by an UW in Chile as well as a short duration overlay.

## Credit

US HG demand defies heavy supply and lower yields. Despite significant supply and lower yields, US HG bond demand remains strong, with JULI spreads only 3bp wider MTD. M&A issuance is on the rise, forecasted to be the highest since 2018 in both dollar terms and as a percentage of total issuance. Despite concerns about US elections and potential slowdowns, deals like the Verizon-Frontier announcement indicate ongoing activity. The BBB versus A spread differential at 41bp is at a 5-month wide indicating some market pricing for downside risks which is likely to persist given the tight level of spreads. The sharp spread sell-off in early August serves as a reminder that recession fears could lead to significant spread widening. While August data post the first week was reassuring, any negative shift in growth data could spark renewed concerns and spreads have plenty of room to move wider (CMOS, Sep 6<sup>th</sup>).

We remain cautious on euro credit overall, due to: i) rising recession risk; ii) weaker technicals; and iii) poor seasonality. The global economy looks vulnerable with real policy rates turning restrictive, recession signals like the Sahm rule and temporary US 2s10s curve un-inversion, and signs of labor market softening. We expect technicals to weaken as issuers front-load deals before the US election, while lower yields could eventually reduce demand. September is historically a weaker month for credit, with spreads widening by an average of 4bp in investment grade and 20bp in high yield since 2012 (European Credit Weekly, Sep 6<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/eea80e9c3fbd5c1eeb6e4f3619f2fc47a355172cde0651ddc7eeedabf7a2d5d8.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

We raise our Euro HY FY24 default rate forecast from 2.5% to 3%, and maintain a 3% target for FY25. Accounting for default losses, credit risk premia are still close to post-GFC lows. This is at odds with the rising probability of a global slowdown, and we look for moderate spread widening to 425bp (from 350bp). Rich valuations can be partly attributed to the short duration of the index. However, this is starting to reverse: average maturity is up 0.25y since April, and we project refinancing will add 0.3y over the coming 6m (High Yield Talking Points, Sep 4<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

The still-impending carry drawdowns and neo-US exceptionalism. While one may quibble on magnitude, we are convincingly in a lower US yields regime. US employment report confirms that longs in funders are appropriate, but the jury is still out on high beta FX as the door to both “landings” remains open. Lower US yields should result in subpar returns from FX carry regardless of where we land as yields are unattractive, Fed easing supports funders, positioning lingers and event risk is high (US elections). Easing US labor markets makes high yielders even more vulnerable.

Trade Recommendations: Scaled up JPY length via 1m USD/JPY put spread. Rotated EUR/JPY short to long-JPY vs EUR & CHF. Continue to fund out of EUR (vs USD, JPY, Scandis). Take loss on long NOK/SEK on Norway CPI risk. CAD shorts can complement short-USD/JPY trades. We stay MW EM FX overall, with OW EMEA EM, small OW LatAm, MW Asia. Favored longs are TRY, ZAR, ILS, MXN; shorts PLN, CZK and COP (FXMW, Sep 6<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Crude inventory draw by 2 mbd since the end of June brings global stocks to their lowest level since 2017, confirming the resilience of global demand. Yet, substantially softer balance in 2025 is forcing the market to search for a price that will prevent OPEC+ from bringing volumes that are not needed. We expect OPEC+ will maintain current production levels at least for another year. At current levels of inventories, we believe oil is \$10 under-valued and under-owned. Yet, given oil’s large underperformance in August, we shaved our 4Q24 price forecast from \$85 to \$80, but keep 2025 unchanged (Oil Markets Weekly, Sep 5<sup>th</sup>). Global oil demand moderates in August, but remains above our estimates, and global observable liquids inventories drew 1.8 mbd in August (Oil Demand & Inventory Tracker, Sep 5<sup>th</sup>).

Global commodity open interest value fell to a six-month low due to contract expirations across the energy and metals complex in the final week of August along with weaker prices. Contract-based outflows (across all trader types) of over -\$29 billion w/w were some of the largest of 2024 YTD, led by crude oil, natural gas, corn, silver and copper markets (as of 30 Aug). Sugar remains our highest conviction long call across ags markets, and we maintain our 24 USc/lb average price forecast through 4Q24 (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Sep 2<sup>nd</sup>).

## BoE’s leap of faith among European peers

BoE recently began easing despite ongoing strength in services and wage growth...

• ...placing faith in alternative and more forward looking indicators that show moderating inflation risk

But UK convergence looks slow on a cross country basis, prompting a cautious easing pace from the BoE

• Sweden stands out at the other end of the spectrum, with very low forward looking inflation metrics

Riksbank has recently gained confidence in controlling inflation, and plans to speed up the pace of easing

In recent months the BoE has looked through exceptional strength in services inflation and wage growth to begin cutting rates. Its justification is that the broader risks around inflation have moderated, and it has cited several more forward looking metrics in support of this. These include a moderation in survey based inflation expectations, PPI inflation and the V/U ratio. These indicators often receive less attention and don’t always accurately track the underlying inflation process, so warrant more scrutiny.

We evaluate the BoE's approach by undertaking a European cross-country analysis. A similar downshift in comparable forward looking measures can be seen everywhere, but the UK stands out. Some of the forward looking indicators are on balance higher in the UK, but more significantly realized inflation appears to be tracking their downshift more slowly. This contrasts most starkly with Sweden, where most inflation indicators have now fallen very sharply. But both in Sweden and the Euro area, inflation is more closely tracking the forward looking metrics which should bring some confidence.

We illustrate the policy implications of these differences using a Taylor Rule framework. In the UK, the MPC is cogni sant of the upside risk we highlight. It only narrowly voted to cut in August and is setting out a slow and cautious pace of easing in its forward guidance. We expect a quarterly pace of cuts while upside inflation risks remain present.

In Sweden, the Riksbank has gained more confidence about bringing inflation under control and appears to be growing somewhat more concerned about downside risk. It recently passed on the option of cutting 50bps at a single meeting. But it has nevertheless guided towards a faster pace of easing in the months ahead, with cuts at every meeting. This is in line with our long held view that the terminal rate would be 2%.

## Alt inflation: a forward looking approach

There is a debate about whether wage growth contains information about future inflation or merely reflects past trends. In Europe the wage process tends to either lag or be more indicative of the current inflation process. Focussing on the year ago change in these measures, we begin by creating what we call a “backward looking” metric defined as the simple average of core consumer price inflation and wage growth (Figure 1). For the sake of comparison, wage growth is standardized so it has the same mean and standard deviation as core inflation itself over the 2001-2019 period. This metric is running at 3.1% in the UK compared to its pre-pandemic pace of 1.8%. While inflation is clearly moderating, these two key components of the inflation process remain elevated. This presents a cause for concern among the hawks on the MPC currently.

![](images/476aa8c18962ffb1696e8f344cd87cfd73a7c4eb3452f31b4d12db605cb76028.jpg)

Figure 2: UK forward looking inflation indicators
%oya, standardized to mean and standard deviation of core inflation, 2001-2019
![](images/e2e7dbcaad10eedf259d66450c08a270f86d827a8debb04b378004e950b71284.jpg)

We also create an alternative and somewhat more “forward looking” inflation metric which we define as the average of the following three components: core PPI inflation, the ratio of vacancies to unemployed, and an average of survey based pricing measures from businesses and consumers. We use the price reading of the PMI for the former, as it can capture underlying inflation trends, and expected year ahead price changes for the latter. These indicators have featured more prominently in the BoE’s commentary recently (see here and here).

For the sake of comparison, each of these inputs are again standardized so they have the same mean and standard deviation as core inflation. We could have included a broader set of indicators, but some have a limited history. As illustrated by Figure 2, each of these inputs has now dropped sharply from their highs. The V/U still lies above its historical average, with PPI inflation just below and inflation expectations close to average. We again agnostically use a simple average to combine these alternative indictors into a single composite, which imposes no theoretical or apriori assumptions.

Figure 3: UK inflation indicators
%oya, standardized to mean and standardization of core inflation, 2001-2019
![](images/5a1673e7f7819cbedc78892a84419390e1bcf5a6d0e0d76e1d00e2d614967906.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The composite of the more forward looking indicators has fallen back to 2.2% in the UK, which is a little high but nevertheless very close to where it had been prior to the pandemic (Figure 3). This is a source of comfort for the doves on the MPC. The main issue the BoE faces is how reliable these forward looking indicators are. The forward looking metric does correlate to some degree with the backward looking metric over time, with an optimal lead of around three months or so. But a gap has opened up between the two in recent years.

The backward looking indictor is running around a percentage point higher at the moment than might have been expected based on the past relationship. There is the risk, therefore, that something more permanent has changed in the inflation process that means core inflation will settle a little above 3%. This is currently a concern for the BoE's hawks, and was highlighted by the Bank in a recent upside scenario for inflation. It may, however, simply be the case that the lags are just a little longer this time, perhaps reflecting the unique features of the pandemic. This would warrant patience on disinflation and present an argument for earlier or faster easing. This seems to be closer to the argument of the doves.

## UK in a European context

What if we applied the same framework to other European countries to assess how unique the BoE’s approach is? We construct comparable measures for both the Euro area and Sweden. The picture for the Euro area is not too dissimilar to the UK, with forward looking indicators close to 2% and a reasonably good correlation with the backward looking metric over time. The historical lag between the two is a fair bit longer than in the UK. But after accounting for this, the relationship does not appear to have broken down since the pandemic as it has done in the UK (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Euro area inflation indicators
%oya, standardized to mean and standard deviation of core inflation 2001-2019
![](images/3d1084ae0c17f54157e425dcd6f9485862c3e94faf0c35ba396ed02f4250a543.jpg)

The picture for Sweden is notable. The relationship between backward and forward looking indicators has been less reliable since around 2018, which questions how much weight should be placed on the forward looking signal (Figure 5). The optimal lag historically has also been longer than in the UK. But the backward looking inflation metric has fallen sharply - mostly due to core inflation - and does now appear to be tracking the path laid out by the forward looking metrics. The path indicates a drop to well below the 2% target, reflecting significant declines in PPI inflation, private sector inflation expectations and the V/U ratio.

Figure 5: Sweden inflation indicators
%oya, standardized to mean and standard deviation of core inflation 2001-2019
![](images/7f279371c9c167f75756c66e37aefcb456ea6fe7b28e2ecd794e041d2dc8d3b3.jpg)

There are several important caveats around this analysis. We use a limited and standardized set of indicators in order to facilitate a cross country comparison. But in some cases a broader set of alternative indicators send more mixed signals. This is the case in Sweden, for example (see 100 charts on Scandi inflation).

Several of the forward looking inputs we use have also dropped back to what might be considered "normal" levels. But in the way we have constructed the metric, this produces a very low inflation estimate as the “norm” prior to the pandemic was a low pace of core inflation (except in the UK). It is possible the norm is higher now. Indeed, even with Sweden’s more rapid inflation progress, some Riksbank board

members have been concerned that a larger degree of inflation salience could mean companies now find it easier to pass on costs to consumers, especially within services. There are also some big differences in the lags we have used for each economy, which have been chosen based on the historical correlation from 2001-2019. If the UK lags are simply longer now than in the past, the BoE can be patient and has less to worry about.

## Divergence in central bank risk biases

Notwithstanding these caveats, both the UK and Sweden stand out for different reasons and this is reflected in recent changes in the risk bias of the respective central banks:

For the UK, backward looking inflation measures continue to run high relative to the alternative and forward looking measures, and to a greater extent than may have been expected based on past lags. This is also evident, for example, in wage growth that continues to run much higher than suggested by the V/U ratio. This leaves the UK standing high compared to other European economies (Figure 6). Upside risk is evident in the BoE’s latest forecasts. Its modal projections present a benign inflation scenario that flags the possibility of a faster pace of easing. But the BoE’s mean projection, which embodies upside risk reflecting a less benign inflation scenario, signals the BoE is intending to trend carefully for now with a quarterly pace of cuts. Our core inflation forecast for next year is on the high side at 3%, and is closer to the BoE’s upside inflation scenario. For it to move more quickly there would need to be greater confidence around the inflation path converging towards the forward looking metric.

For Sweden, if the signal from the forward looking inflation indicator were to prove accurate, it would likely prompt a more dovish response from a central bank. There are several reasons to be cautious, however. The forward looking indicators in Sweden have been less reliable since around 2018, and generally have a weaker correlation with inflation than other economies. The Riksbank sees inflation risks around its target as balanced currently. It assumes a 2.5-3.0% estimate of neutral rates, and may be hesitant to ease much below this level absent a recession. There are also other important factors that could slow the Riksbank: expansionary fiscal policy, Sweden’s high interest rate sensitivity - which implies that relatively few cuts could quickly stimulate demand - and currency considerations if it were to ease more significantly than other central banks. That said, the Riksbank recently considered a 50bp cut. We do not expect it to move in larger clips, but it has recently acknowledged an increase in downside risk and is guiding towards a somewhat faster pace of cuts this year.

Figure 6: Gap between backward and forward looking indicators %oya, adjusted for pre-pandemic lead of the forward looking indicators
![](images/90db3973259e84360386ff584291dc13dadd453abe254c41c52e21b05ec634eb.jpg)

## Illustrated with a Taylor Rule application

Another way of illustrating this risk bias is through the lens of a Taylor Rule. We use the balanced estimates recently published by our global team (see here, Table 2) as a starting point. We use our in house projections and focus on medium term estimates for 4Q25. The balanced Taylor rule estimates on that horizon are reasonably similar to our policy rate projections (blue and green bars, Figure 7). As an alternative, we assume core inflation perfectly follows the path laid out by the forward looking indicators over time and settles there, i.e. we substitute these two variables in the Taylor rule estimate. Doing so opens up particular downside scenarios on rates for the BoE and Riksbank (Figure 7, yellow bars).

However, if we take the additional step of incorporating information on how well the forward looking metrics are tracking the observed data at present, the picture changes notably for the UK (Figure 7, orange lines). The upside risk evident in the UK case pushes the Taylor Rule estimates back up close to our official forecast of 3.75%. For Sweden, it leaves a still low policy rate projection of close to 1%. This highlights the downside risk discussed earlier. The Riksbank is responding to this risk by planning to ease more quickly than previously envisaged. But for the reasons listed above, we have so far maintained our terminal rate forecast of 2% for next year and therefore expect the Riksbank to get there a little earlier than before.

Figure 7: Taylor Rule estimates of policy rate for end-2025
![](images/833ff426822f4e64c9545ec06e3f580fff6a8f87ad65e6fb5b23b691db408806.jpg)

## Australia: Hoarded labor creates a downside risk

Compared to prior cycles, more of the cyclical decline in total hours reflects falls in hours per worker

• This appears to reflect a labor hoarding dynamic

• Unemployment likely to be more sensitive should labor demand fall now

In absolute terms, hours worked in Australia remain little changed from the 2023 highs. If we de-trend the data, to give a better sense of cyclical adjustments, hours worked have fallen about 3% since their 1Q23 peak. Relative to previous cycles, significantly more of the adjustment in (de-trended) hours worked has come through declines in hours per worker (Figure 1). In contrast, the cyclical decline in employed individuals has been more muted than in the past (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Cumulative decline in hours per worker since hours peak Index, cyclical peak = 100
![](images/5ab714feaab75aeb161cb7564908e6faa6c9977ad06bc923f14d2ab5a1f31992.jpg)

Figure 2: Cumulative decline in employment since hours peak
![](images/0e30e742a0d3730cd46cb80cc8855d80e63dfcae4657aa366059f45fa06fabbc.jpg)

Labor hoarding might explain why the hours adjustment has mainly occurred through hours per worker. Hiring and firing workers is costly. And when there is uncertainty about the persistence of shocks to economic activity, firms may prefer to initially hold onto staff and later adjust employment, when they have more clarity about the outlook. If labour adjustment costs have risen, or firms are facing greater uncertainty, the hoarding dynamic might be more pronounced this cycle.

Changes in recruitment/hiring costs are more likely to reflect long-run structural change in the economy, so it’s hard to put the recent step change in employment/hours down to a sharp rise in labor adjustment costs. But there is some evidence from business surveys that uncertainty is currently elevated.

Using the Westpac-ACCI survey, we proxy uncertainty by examining differences between the proportion of firms that expect to increase and decrease employment in the next three months. In general, uncertainty will be higher, the closer these proportions are to a 50/50 split<sup>1</sup> . Figure 3 shows that uncertainty surrounding business hiring intentions is somewhat elevated relative to 2016-19 and the pandemic, so it’s possible firms are postponing retrenchment decisions until the outlook becomes clearer. The experience of the pandemic, when policymakers were able to smooth through large shocks, may also now make firms more likely to perceive shocks as temporary, adding to the hoarding dynamic.

Figure 3: Uncertainty in business hiring intentions
![](images/dc89616f25d238ce8e47890d499213ebfde7409d30f240bd60a6a18f3c97178f.jpg)

## Shifts and shares

There are other ways to rationalise recent employment/hours outcomes, aside from hoarding. Public sector employment growth has outperformed the private sector over the past year. While this is common in periods in which the labour market is loosening, it’s also possible that employment is rotating from industries with higher average hours per worker to industries with lower hours per worker, bringing down hours per worker in the aggregate figures. To get a sense of the effects of industry composition on hours per worker, we construct a counterfactual hours per worker series over 1Q23- 2Q24. We fix hours per worker of each industry as at 1Q23, but allow each industry’s share of total employment, which we use as weights, to evolve in line with the data over the following five quarters.

Figure 4: Decomposition of hours per worker Change in hours relative to 1Q23
![](images/c7a09a8fbc74f1b3f52edaf91336f4e45df300713480844745d74735ab4314b3.jpg)
Feb 23 May 23 Aug 23 Nov 23 Feb 24 May 24 Aug 24 Nov 24 Source: J.P. Morgan

Figure 4 decomposes changes in hours per worker into the industry effect – the part which reflects changes in the distribution of workers across industries – and a within industry effect – the part which reflects changes in hours per worker in each given industry. The decomposition shows that changes in labor force composition have not contributed to the decline in hours per worker that has occurred since 1Q23. In fact, changes in the distribution of workers across industries have pushed aggregate hours per worker the other way.

The results make it hard to characterise recent hours dynamics as arising from shifts in the industry composition of employment. The within-industry effect driving the decline in hours per worker might reflect factors such as full-time workers shifting to part-time work, which would be consistent with a labor hoarding dynamic. Though we don’t find a great deal of evidence that the rate at which full-time workers are transitioning to part-time is particularly elevated. Figure 5 shows the proportion of workers moving from full-time to part-time work has picked up, but largely remains below levels from 2013 onward. This means hours declines have occurred mostly within the given full-time/part-time categories.

Figure 5: Proportion of full-time workers moving to part time
![](images/0e32f5ce2b104867f62f199b5c0d827ee28a3aee40e49f502d8a176f56897305.jpg)

## Why does it matter, anyway?

We care about hoarding to the extent that it increases the risk that the unemployment rate rises more sharply from here: if firms are hoarding labor in the face of uncertainty, and that uncertainty resolves, then firms may shed workers more

aggressively. But, even with a concrete measure of hoarding, there’s a large degree of behavioral uncertainty that makes it hard to map hoarding behavior to the unemployment rate.

Labor hoarding aside, there are good reasons to think unemployment will be more sensitive to falls in labor demand from here. At last month’s Jackson Hole conference, Benigno and Eggertsson showed the US unemployment rate is less/more sensitive to changes in vacancies above/below a threshold ratio of the vacancy to unemployment rate (the Beveridge threshold). In previous work, we estimated an equilibrium unemployment rate based on the Beveridge threshold in the Australian context. Figure 6 plots that threshold, and the actual vacancy and unemployment rates over 80Q1-24Q2.

Figure 6: Australian beveridge curve and threshold Vacancy rate, %
![](images/988654f5d0d0f9634d1186f9c35cd008b2bae39a3fac323a2a3befaac2582669.jpg)

As in the US, Figure 6 shows the relationship between the unemployment and vacancy rates is markedly steeper above the Beveridge threshold. The upshot is that most of the cooling in labor demand so far has manifested as falls in vacancies rather than a rise in unemployment. However, as of 2Q24, the labor market is operating to the right of the threshold, implying that further falls in vacancies should be associated with a larger rise in the unemployment rate.

We continue to expect only a modest rise in the unemployment rate (4Q24: 4.4%), though this is mainly predicated on better growth prospects, which will support labor demand in turn. The move towards a flatter segment of the Beveridge curve however maintains the risk of more significant rises in the unemployment rate.

Even though recent RBA communications have screened hawkish, by characterising inflation persistence as resulting from stubborn capacity pressures the Bank has preserved optionality to ease policy if the labor market softens more substantially than forecast. This would not be the case if the Bank had pinned inflation pressures on movements in inflation expectations, which might require tight policy to re-anchor, even in the face of weaker labor market outcomes.

## United States

The August employment report showed ongoing moderation in job growth

The unemployment rate ticked down from 4.3% to 4.2% as a spike in temporary layoffs reversed

We continue to look for a 50bps cut at the September FOMC meeting, but we expect FOMC members will actively debate 25bps and 50bps options

• Next week brings August inflation reports: we look for a 0.2%m/m increase in core CPI

This week brought more signs of a gradually cooling labor market, though it left enough uncertainty about the pace of deterioration that we expect the FOMC will seriously debate whether a 25bps or 50bps cut at the September meeting is appropriate. We still think cutting 50bp is the right thing to do. Policy is restrictive, downside employment risks are growing, and upside inflation risks are ebbing. Fed speakers agree that multiple cuts are warranted, though they haven’t openly endorsed catching up to the curve as soon as possible. So, while we are still calling for a 50bp cut at the next meeting as we think it’s good policy, we don’t have full confidence that the Committee agrees just yet.

Figure 1: Nonfarm employment growth
![](images/ebffc1edcf8dbe3959fd312c6c2626cc1c88c449cf3ad41600ea8322b19f8090.jpg)

The key piece of news this week was the August employment report. Nonfarm employment rose 142k for the month, and there was a total 86k downward revision to the prior two months. That brought the three-month moving average down to 116k (private industry 96k), which is notably weak even compared to the slow growth in the 2010s expansion. A few months ago one could argue the trend hadn’t softened that much since the start of 2023, but the latest numbers suggest that a gradual slowing in the pace of payroll growth is continuing. Moreover, the preliminary benchmark estimates for payrolls showed that gains from March 2023 to March 2024 could eventually be revised down 70k per month, all in private payrolls. While that doesn’t necessarily imply there will also be downward revisions to more recent months, FOMC members may still be concerned that recent readings could

later be marked lower.

Looking to the household survey, the unemployment rate retreated from 4.3% to 4.2%, though considering the unrounded figures the decline was just 3bps. The small drop in the unemployment rate mainly stemmed from a reversal of a July spike in people unemployed because of a temporary layoff, while the number of people unemployed for other reasons rose. Some of the upward pressure on the unemployment rate continues to stem from a solid labor supply as the sum of unemployed new entrants and re-entrants kept rising. The labor force participation rate held steady at 62.7%, and while prime-age participation did edge down 0.1%-pt it remains just shy of its all-time highs.

![](images/92eb2fb8822b191aa26995c7923920c14819ca4e136367b66aa5dfe6f08d99b7.jpg)

Elsewhere in the establishment survey, the workweek rebounded to 34.3 from 34.2, and average hourly earnings rose a solid 0.4%m/m (3.8%oya). Nonetheless, through the first two months of the quarter nominal labor income is expanding at a 3.9% annual rate, the softest quarter of the expansion by over a percentage point. Slower income growth could prove a headwind to consumer spending growth, which has been the engine powering the economy in recent quarters. There were also further signs that industries that have been key for recent payroll gains were slowing or or had already settled into a softer trend. Healthcare and social assistance jobs growth has moderated for the last three months straight, and the 44k gain in August was just over half the 81k/month pace in the prior year. Government employment also rose just 24k, continuing what has been a much slower pace of gains since April. Since then payroll growth has averaged just 14k/ month, whereas in the year to March it was rising 55k/month.

In addition to the August employment report this week brought the July JOLTS, which also showed less job market vigor. The job opening rate slid to 4.6% from 4.8% the prior month, and the ratio of openings to unemployed declined to its lowest level since early 2018. The decline in this ratio should reduce worries at the FOMC that the labor market is still too tight and further shift the focus from inflation to labor risks.

Michael Feroli (1-212) 834-5523 michael.e.feroli@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank NA Michael S Hanson (1-212) 622-8603 michael.s.hanson@jpmchase.com

Abiel Reinhart (1-212) 270 4058abiel.reinhart@jpmchase.com

The FOMC will see one more set of inflation reports before their next meeting, with the August CPI and PPI being released next week. We are not looking for any surprises here, with our forecast being that the core CPI will increase 0.2%m/m, which would keep the over-year-ago rate at 3.2%. That outcome, if realized, would likely be interpreted by the Fed as one consistent with inflation continuing to move back to target.

With the next FOMC meeting coming up on September 17- 18, today was the last day for Fed speakers to communicate their policy stance before the blackout period begins. In providing his view on the outlook, Governor Waller was clear that there were labor market risks that would require cutting rates, though he focused on the role of labor force entrants in raising unemployment, stating that the increase “appears to be more of a supply-side-driven phenomenon, not demand driven.” Consequently he cautioned against overreliance on measures like the Sahm rule, noting that “many pre-pandemic economic relationships have not proven to be good policy guides post-pandemic.” Nonetheless, he concluded that he was “open-minded about the size and pace of cuts” and that he would be an advocate of front-loading rate cuts “if that is appropriate,” a signal that larger rate cuts will be considered. NY Fed President Williams also spoke but said less of note about the size or pace of cuts. Finally, Chicago Fed President Goolsbee, who is not a FOMC voting member, was dovish, remarking that “there are definite warning signs of things over-cooling” in an interview.

## A darker shade of Beige Book

In addition to the regular survey data, the Fed’s September Beige Book also provided qualitative insight into labor market developments. The overall summary, that “employment levels were generally flat to up slightly in recent weeks,” was little different from the last survey in July, although new was the mention that “a few Districts reported that firms reduced shifts and hours, left advertised positions unfilled, or reduced head counts through attrition—though accounts of layoffs remained rare.” The lack of layoffs was consistent with the JOLTS, whose layoff rate continues to move sideways.

While the overall assessment of conditions for the nation was similar, the details were softer. The survey didn’t highlight any sharp adjustment in activity, but most districts’ labor market assessments were slightly weaker than in July, with some firms reducing hiring plans or finding it easier to hire as applicant levels increased.

## Manufacturing surveys look soft, services more mixed

The August PMI and ISM manufacturing surveys are now running at levels that are only barely above cycle lows, having given up some modest gains early in the year, highlighting the challenges in that sector. Moreover, the ISM production index slid to its lowest reading since the pandemic. Even as GDP has been steadily expanding, domestic manufacturing has been soft, with almost no change in employment and lower industrial production over the last couple years.

The business surveys were more mixed on the non-manufacturing side. The PMI services business activity index edged up in August, is at its highest level since early 2022, and is not particularly weak compared to its own history, which feels consistent with the ongoing gains in GDP this year. In contrast, the ISM survey’s business activity measure has largely drifted steadily down over the last couple years and is on the low end of what would be expected in a growing economy. However, both surveys report soft employment measures, which aligns with the gradual slowing in job growth.

## Construction spending

Nominal construction spending declined 0.3%m/m in July, the first drop in almost two years, consistent with our expectation that real private structures investment is set to be soft this quarter. Our forecast remains that real residential investment will fall 10%q/q saar and that private nonresidential structures investment will be flat. Declines in construction spending in the prior couple months were revised away on stronger home improvement spending, which had appeared to be stabilizing in 2Q but now appears to be continuing an earlier upward trend. While that data doesn’t feed the GDP accounts, other data that does, including building supply store retail sales and residential remodeler income, were up in July and suggest improvements will continue to offset some of the weakness in new home construction. On the nonresidential side, growth in manufacturing has slowed and was outweighed by declines in other types of construction.

Figure 3: Private residential construction spending
![](images/4d06412f2678ba28adece03ae1b8942f2719f293e7b670b1d10fe618985f35b5.jpg)

We estimate that the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.2% in August – similar to its pace of increase in July, which also was close to the cusp between rounding down to 0.1% and rounding up to 0.2%. Energy prices are expected to have weighed on the headline CPI last month, with fairly broadbased weakness across energy prices for consumers leading to a -0.7% decline in August. We see food prices in the CPI continuing to run at roughly the same 0.2% monthly pace they have exhibited in recent months. On a year-ago basis, we see headline CPI inflation cooling to 2.6%, which would be its softest annual reading since March 2021.

Away from food and energy, we expect the closely-watched core CPI measure rose 0.21% in August, which would be the firmest print in four months. If realized, year-ago core CPI inflation would have held steady at 3.2% in August. Within the details for core CPI, shelter-related prices have been volatile in recent months, but we look for both owner’s equivalent rent (OER) and tenants’ rent to return to their prior gradual cooling trend, whereas lodging prices are likely to have fallen again in August after a brief reversal in July of a sustained downward trend. We also look for a relatively firm reading on airfares that should boost the contribution of public transportation prices to core CPI. Medical prices should also rise in August after a surprising decline in medical services prices in July that we expect to be short-lived.

Among the other key components of the CPI, industry data suggest that used vehicle prices are likely to post another sizable decline in August, albeit less than half as large as July’s decline. Separate industry data point to some modest rebound in new vehicle prices as well. The remaining CPI categories are generally forecast to have increased modestly in August, with an exception for a slight decline in communication

goods and services.
<table><tr><td</table>

We expect that initial jobless claims were 225k in the week ending September 7, largely unchanged form 227k the prior week. After peaking at 241k the four-week average of claims has been slowly trending down even as the labor market appears to be softening. Jobless claims have been tracking quite similar to last year on a not seasonally adjusted basis, and with similar seasonal factors to last year it is possible that jobless claims will decline on a seasonally adjusted basis like they did last September unless the labor market weakens more substantially.

Continuing claims meanwhile fell 22k in the week ending August 24, and after a substantial rise in June continuing claims appear to have been edging down since late July. This is good news, though relative stability in continuing claims over the last year hasn’t stopped the unemployment rate from increasing substantially. Interestingly, even as continuing claims have remained fairly stable, the exhaustion rate for continuing claims has kept steadily rising. The exhaustion rate is defined as the number of claims making their final payment at 26 weeks of normal eligibility, divided by the number of first payments six months earlier. We then average that over the trailing 12 months to eliminate seasonality and reduce noise. That rate rose to 38.0% in July from 34.5% a year earlier. While higher exhaustions will all else equal reduce the number of continuing claims, they have tended to rise together in the past since both increase with a worsening labor market. Moreover, the exhaustion rate is rising despite there being relatively little increase either in the share of unemployment lasting 27 weeks or longer or in the mean and median duration of unemployment. This could suggest that the takeup of unemployment insurance, which is around historical lows, is more concentrated among individuals with less likelihood of being re-employed.

We estimate that the producer price index (PPI) rose 0.1% in August, similar to the pace recorded for July. A -1.1% pullback in energy prices and an unchanged monthly reading for food prices are expected to contribute to a soft headline reading last month. Away from food and energy prices, we look for the core PPI to have risen 0.2% in August, with both core goods and core services PPI prices to have posted similar paces of monthly gains. Meanwhile, construction prices are expected to be flat on the month after a strong rise in July. The year-ago PPI inflation rate continues to moderate, and if our forecasts are realized, the headline reading would decline to 1.7% from 2.2% in July. This would mark the softest annual pace of PPI inflation since February. The core PPI inflation rate has been choppy in recent months around a modestly firming trend; we look for the year-ago rate to inch up to 2.5% in August from 2.4% the month prior.

We expect the import price index to be unchanged in the month of August. Since May, the import price index has changed little month to month. A decline in imported energy prices is expected to more than offset a small rise in imported food prices to help hold down the headline index. Meanwhile, we look for the nonfuel import price index to have risen 0.2% last month. We anticipate more firmness in some of the underlying components, such as imported capital goods and autos, but broadly flat for imported consumer goods excluding autos. On a year-ago basis, the headline import price index is expected to cool to a four-month low of 1.0%, whereas the year-ago growth of the nonfuel import price index should show a modest acceleration to 1.5% – a 21-month high.

Index (nsa)</td><td>68.2</td><td>66.4</td><td>67.9</td><td>68.0</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>65.9</td><td>62.7</td><td>61.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>69.6</td><td>68.8</td><td>72.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.0</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

We expect the consumer sentiment index in the preliminary Michigan survey for September will remain about unchanged at 68.0. There are some clear positives over the last few weeks that could contribute positively to sentiment, including lower gas prices and falling mortgage rates, and equity prices are still up from their recent early August trough. At the same time we think that labor market conditions are slowly softening, and this could create an offsetting drag on sentiment.

Sentiment had increased last month by 1.5pts on higher expectations (assessments of current conditions were down), a rise that appeared related to the flurry of election news since Joe Biden withdrew from the presidential race. Sentiment had fallen among Democrats in July in response to improving polling for Donald Trump, then snapped back in August as Kamala Harris gained in the polls. There have been fewer sharp swings in the election polls recently, so we suspect that there won’t be as much of a sentiment boost this month from political developments.

## Review of past week’s data

The manufacturing PMI and ISM surveys continued to exhibit soft readings in August and are at levels that are on the low side of what would normally be seen in a growing economy. Both surveys have improved earlier in the year and since then have retreated near their cycle lows, although since the improvement wasn’t that large to begin with the recent softening also isn’t that significant and doesn’t suggest a sharp deterioration in activity. For the PMI, the final August reading (47.9) was hardly changed from the flash survey (48.0) and is down from 49.6 in July. For the ISM, the composite was 47.2 in August vs. 46.8 in July and down from the recent high of 50.3 in March.

Measures of new orders and production softened across both surveys between July and August. The message here is mostly similar to the headline: weaker than earlier in the year, though slightly better than cycle lows. The one measure that stands out is ISM production, which has declined for five straight months and at 44.8 is weaker than at any other time post-COVID. The index did fall to similar levels in late 2019, but this index is very much on the end of non-recession readings.

Employment readings across the two surveys were mixed between July and August but on balance are weak. For the PMI, employment has fallen over the last couple months and is now almost back at the cycle lows from 4Q23. On the other hand, the ISM measure, which had already fallen to a cycle low of 43.4 in July, rebounded to 46.0 in August, though looking past the monthly volatility it still appears that this measure could be slowly drifting lower.

Inventory measures were also mixed across the surveys. For the PMI, the stocks of inputs index edged down in August, and S&P characterized recent readings on this index as consistent with stocks falling for six straight months. On the other hand, while the stocks of finished goods index did also decline, it remains near historical highs. The ISM inventories index meanwhile had been quite low in recently but jumped 5.8 points to 50.3 in August. According to the ISM, a reading above 44.4 is usually consistent with rising inventories in the GDP report.

## Construction spending (Sep 3)

<table><tr><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td><td>Jul</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Nominal</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Private</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Residential</td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.8</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Nonresidential</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Public</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr></table>

Construction spending declined 0.3%m/m in July as spending on new residential construction dropped again (-1.5%) and private nonresidential construction, whose growth has recently slowed, also fell 0.4%. Total spending for the prior couple months was revised up significantly though, mostly from residential improvement spending. Improvements had previously flattened in 2Q, but after the revisions they look to be continuing the rapid gains seen since late last year. This won’t lead to revisions in 2Q GDP as the GDP accounts use different source data. Residential improvement spending in this report comes from the Consumer Expenditure Survey, whereas the GDP report uses data related to building supply store sales and residential remodeler payrolls. Those series have also been trending up, which should lead to residential improvements offsetting some of the declines in new residential construction. We expect weakness in new residential construction to persist for some months based on past declines in housing starts, and we look for total real residential investment in the GDP accounts to fall 10%q/q saar in 3Q.

For private nonresidential construction, spending outside of manufacturing continues to decline, driven by commercial construction, which is not being offset as much recently by growth in manufacturing spending. Manufacturing spending has grown rapidly because of high-tech facilities, including semiconductor investment spurred by the CHIPS Act, and it now has the greatest spend of any nonresidential category. Spending ex manufacturing fell 0.6%m/m whereas manufacturing was flat. While manufacturing growth has slowed, it is still up 6% saar over the last six months in real terms.

Looking finally at public construction, this segment was up just 0.1%m/m in July and is up only 1% in the last six months. Following a plunge after COVID real government gross structures investment is now close to being back in line with the pre-pandemic growth trend, so it is probably natural for growth in public structures investment to slow some.

The July JOLTS pointed to further cooling in the labor market as the number of job openings continued to slide, and the ratio of openings to unemployed started to dip below the prepandemic level, falling to its lowest reading since 2018. This should further focus the Fed’s attention on labor market concerns at the next FOMC meeting. While the hiring and quit rates did increase, they remain at low levels after steadily trending down through the prior month. Layoffs meanwhile were still low, consistent with insufficient hiring being a more important cause of the recent rise in the unemployment rate than higher layoffs.

Job openings fell 3.0%m/m in July to 7.673mm from a downward revised 7.910mm in June (previous 8.184mm), and the job openings rate fell to 4.6% from 4.8%. We had expected

Michael Feroli (1-212) 834-5523 michael.e.feroli@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank NA Michael S Hanson (1-212) 622-8603 michael.s.hanson@jpmchase.com

Abiel Reinhart (1-212) 270 4058abiel.reinhart@jpmchase.com

that job openings would have remained roughly unchanged month over month given stability in some other measures of job openings, including Indeed’s daily job postings and job openings among small businesses in the NFIB survey, but the JOLTS measure defied this and appears to be on a steady downward trajectory. Openings were down across a wide range of industries. Government, which had reported a sharp run-up in openings in the prior couple months, saw some of that increase revised away for June and then pull back in July, while the hiring rate kept trending down. That could suggest that the recent slowdown in government payroll growth will continue. The opening rate for construction also fell sharply for a second consecutive month, declining from 4.3% in May to 2.9% in July, which excluding the pandemic is the lowest since early 2018. Construction employment has been robust despite a falling number of housing units under construction and some recent softness in nonresidential construction, and the decline in openings may signal that this will start to translate into a more material jobs slowdown soon.

With openings down in July and unemployment rising, the openings/unemployed ratio fell from 1.16 to 1.07. The Fed has looked to this measure as a sign of how balanced the labor market is, and with it falling below pre-pandemic levels, there should be less concern about tightness in labor conditions. This measure may also start to get less attention: when inflation was higher the normalization of the ratio was seen as an important benchmark to judge labor market balance, whereas now, the focus will likely lean toward more traditional measures like the unemployment rate.

The layoff and discharges rate did increase to 1.1% in July from 1.0% in June (previous 0.9%), but the rate has been oscillating between 1.0% and 1.1% for the last year and there is no sign of this drifting upward. These are low rates, and other indicators of layoffs, including initial jobless claims, WARN Act notices, and announced layoffs reported by Challenger, Gray, and Christmas, have also been subdued. Consequently, layoffs still do not appear to be a major driver of the increase in the unemployment rate so far, with lower hiring amid rising labor supply a more pertinent factor.

On the hiring front, the hires rate did increase to 3.5% from 3.3%, but this comes after a sustained decline over prior months, with the June reading having been the lowest since 2014 (ex the pandemic), so it is not clear if there is yet any stabilization here. The quit rate also rose to 2.1% from 2.0%, but as with hires this comes on the back of substantial prior declines, with June being the lowest since early 2018 (ex the pandemic).

The PMI and ISM services surveys for August again sent somewhat contrasting signals about the direction of travel in the economy, though they do find more agreement in soft labor measures. For general business activity and new orders, the PMI has slowly improved this year, and readings are not particularly weak relative to history. In contrast, the ISM, while little changed between July and August, has softened a bit over the course of the last year and is on the low end of what would normally be seen in an expansion. The more optimistic message from the PMI has been more consistent with the solid GDP growth this year.

The ISM services composite was largely unchanged in August, edging up only to 51.5 from 51.4 in July and the cycle low of 48.8 in June. New orders improved slightly to 53.0 from 52.4, whereas business activity fell to 53.3 from 54.5, but the three-month average of both of these series is the lowest in the post-pandemic period and is low relative to a longer history. Meanwhile employment declined to 50.2 from 51.1. Employment was quite weak earlier in the year, averaging 47.7 in 1H24 before rebounding in July. While the latest reading is low compared to a longer history, similar to general activity and new orders, it’s not that different from the 2022- 2023 average of a 50.6.

For the PMI, the headline business activity index increased slightly to 55.7 in the final August reading from 55.2 in the flash report and 55.0 in July. This measure has often run below the equivalent ISM measure, but the PMI has outperformed the ISM in recent months. The new business index also improved between July and August, whereas the employment index was down sharply. Similar to the ISM, the employment index is low relative to history, but unlike the ISM, the index is also low relative to the post-COVID period and is close to the cycle low set in April.

The August employment report was fairly close to expectations and suggested gradual cooling in the labor market but no sudden break in conditions. Total payrolls rose 142k, private payrolls increased 118k, and the unemployment rate declined from 4.3% to 4.2% (unrounded it was a 0.03%-pt fall, from 4.25% to 4.22%) while participation stayed flat at 62.7%. The number of unemployed on temporary layoff, which had surged in July, pulled back in August, while unemployment rose from other types of job losers and new entrants. The prior two months payrolls were also revised down by a collective 86k and the most recent three months average now stands at 116k/month (96k private). The workweek recovered to 34.3 from 34.2, and average hourly earnings was up a robust 0.4%m/m.

# Focus: The payroll effects of lower immigration

As we discussed earlier this year, immigration has been an unusually large contributor recently to US population growth, which in turn raised breakeven payroll growth to around 200k/month. Much of this increase has come via immigrants who are neither legal permanent residents nor nonimmigrant visa holders. Slowing immigration from this “other” group suggests it’s recent contribution to employment growth is still similar to the 2023 average but is down 35k from the late 2023 peak and could decline another 40k in 2025 if immigration continues to fall.

We follow the CBO’s definition of the “other” immigrant category to include those who (a) entered the country illegally, either by avoiding detection or because they were released by Customs and Border Protection (CBP), (b) those who are lawfully paroled into the country, for example via humanitarian parole, and (c) those who have overstayed their visa. We focus on the first two groups since they are more readily measurable and likely more variable.

The most common metric for tracking changes in growth of the other immigrant group is monthly CBP encounters (Figure 1). To get the subset of these that are adults of working age, we sum all single adults and half the individuals in family units. To then exclude people who are immediately deported, we multiply this count by the fraction of total encounters in each month that result in the individual being given a notice to appear (NTA) in immigration court and/or parole. Many of these encounters will lead to the automatic filing of an immigration court case, which is another useful metric for tracking this type of immigration.

Figure 1: Measures of "other" immigration 000s per month
![](images/c0671f8c0a21cef2ce0ac21dca7438159d6f364fb299a4cd3ad25d981d5975c5.jpg)

These measures fell in early 2023, then declined further in June and July following an executive order that bars migrants crossing the southern border unlawfully from applying for asylum. Our estimate of the number of released adults in July was more than 50k below the 2023 average, and the number of new court cases was about 75k lower.

Not all of these migrants will add to the labor force. Individuals who come into the country via humanitarian parole are immediately eligible to apply for work authorization, while anyone filing for asylum in immigration court can apply 150 days after filing. However, DHS has noted that among migrants who had been paroled after making an appointment via their CBP One app, only a small percentage were subsequently applying for work authorization. Our assumption, however, is that a majority of people are participating in the labor force even when they lack formal work authorization.

Recent noncitizen, working-age immigrants in the Current Population Survey in 2023-2024 had an employment rate close to 60%, so a 50k decline in immigration would reduce employment growth by roughly 30k/month (this is more likely to be reflected in the establishment than the household survey). However, new immigrant employment rates rise over 15% within the first two years of being in the country, so to more accurately map monthly immigration flows into individual monthly payroll contributions we use the larger annual American Community Survey. We compute employment rates for working-age, non-citizen immigrants in the 2022 ACS by year since immigration (using the pre-COVID 2019 ACS would be similar), interpolate these employment rates into months, and then multiply these rates by past immigrant inflows. Finally, we take the difference in the sum of these past values and smooth this with a three-month moving average to compute the incremental impact on payroll growth of changes in the other immigration category.

Figure 2: Additional contribution of "other" immigration to payrolls 000s/month, 3mma, relative to a baseline with 20k/month other immigration
![](images/12e43814e6ca49edadb0bfc526807296978aa3bb75b3c8be695c42692f01b69f.jpg)

The results suggest that, relative to a baseline in which other immigration is only 20k per month, actual immigration boosted monthly payrolls by an average of about 65k in 2023, close to 100k in late 2023 and early 2024, and that by July this fell to a bit over 60k (Figure 2). It would stay mostly constant if immigration didn’t fall further, or decline another 40k by 2025 if immigration gradually returns to pre-2019 levels. The effect could be larger if the number of migrants who cross the border without being detected is also falling.

## Euro area

• ECB almost certain to cut rates by 25bp next week

• Staff forecasts to still show core inflation moving to target

• Latest news on GDP deflator, wages and profits reinforces broader disinflation process

Household spending and Germany struggle to grow, but GDP still grew 1.2%ar in 1H24 ex-Ireland

The ECB is almost certain to cut by 25bp next week and signal further quarterly moves. The latter will be implicit as the ECB will remain data-dependent and will not pre-commit. But, new staff forecasts will show inflation moving back to target, with quarterly cuts factored in through the technical assumptions.

The near-term staff forecast for core inflation should get revised a tick higher due to recent upside surprises giving a higher starting point. But the downward direction on core inflation was reinforced by this week’s data on the GDP deflator, wages and profits, which showed faster-than-expected progress. Productivity remains weak, but has stopped contracting in 1H24, while downside surprises on effective pay are also pulling unit labour cost growth lower. Combined with a shift to stagnating unit profits (at high levels), the GDP deflator growth has more than halved to 3%oya. For now, this progress in the broader price environment will help to override concerns about the stickiness of services inflation, at least sufficiently to reduce the level of monetary restriction a bit further, but more progress will still be needed later on.

That growth risks have shifted to the downside also gives space to move on rates. The economy grew at a 1.2%ar in 1H24, excluding Ireland, but its mix has disappointed as the strong pickup in real incomes is failing to lift consumer spending and capex also edged lower in 2Q due to weakness in Germany. Both factors could prove more persistent and hence weigh on the outlook.

But, despite recent disappointments, consumers remain well supported by strong gains in real incomes and resilient labour markets, and disentangling structural, cyclical and data quality issues in Germany is hard. The car sector certainly faces structural challenges, but the latest data nevertheless hint at a pickup in car production. That has not helped the July IP report, which was very weak, but holiday effects should reverse in August, suggesting that the German economy is close to stagnant rather than in contraction. And, other parts of the region continue to hold up better, which helps to limit the extent of the downside risks for the Euro area as a whole.

## ECB staff forecasts and the latest data

This week’s final GDP report for 2Q24 has come too late to be factored into the staff projections, but nevertheless offers an important update on the ECB’s narrative.

On growth, the challenge is clear. The economy grew at a 1.2%ar in 1H24, once the volatile and distorted data from Ireland are excluded. But, the details showed disappointment in terms of consumer spending, which contracted slightly in 2Q24, despite recent strength in real incomes. And capex is being held back by Germany. Combined with the recent softening in the business surveys, this implies downside risks to growth.

At the same time, further progress was made in terms of the cost and price data in the national accounts. The GDP deflator slowed further to 3.0%oya in 2Q24, less than half its peak pace and lower than the ECB staff projection from June (Figure 1). A key factor behind this was a faster slowing in labor compensation, which not only reflected a temporary downward distortion in Germany (Figure 2). In particular, the German data were distorted by the timing of when one-offs were paid in the retail sector. This slipped into 3Q24 and the latest data already show a pickup into 3Q24, but which should still leave Euro area compensation on a downward trend (Figure 3).

Figure 1: Euro area GDP deflator %oya
![](images/266f59befa5be7422dd6e46f8e230fb8f3704845bee661096a90dec25d6f9e96.jpg)

Figure 2: Euro area compensation per employee
![](images/ba1015c8d07311def78e6cb9685390e4bdb0731bcc543423b8de6f0b2cf4f169.jpg)

Figure 3: German pay settlements
![](images/f839f90c03f86f81985bef75666d1f4555fccdd2f1c1cb4ac05c8464adf7c504.jpg)
Source: WSI-Tarifarchiv, Bundesbank, J.P. Morgan

Another important factor contributing to the slowdown in the GDP deflator is unit profits. These had contributed a lot to the earlier pickup, but have moved to stagnation (Figure 4). The level of unit profits is still high, but it is no longer a driver of inflation. In fact, by stagnating, it is a drag on the GDP deflator. This is in line with the last ECB staff forecast, but is nevertheless encouraging. All of this should provides some reassurance on the stickiness in services inflation, although progress on this will be important if the ECB is to cut rates well into next year.

Figure 4: Euro area unit profits
![](images/b6b66917530442c44c165a8f5ccb332e4e28d75fb17795bd3fdf1339b1276fdb.jpg)

## Another round of German noise

This week has also brought another round of German industry data. The trends remain weak, with surveys signalling contraction. At the same time, the swings likely reflect holidayrelated distortions. This was most clear in the contrast between a very weak July IP report that had manufacturing down 3.2%m/m and the VDA data that showed a jump in car production in August (Figure 5). That jump also contrasts with news that a German carmaker may close some production sites in Germany. While that news is not concrete yet, it highlights structural challenges that may not however prevent a near-term cyclical improvement. That order backlogs are still very high supports this, but unfortunately the German manufacturing surveys remain weak for now. As a result, the near-term outlook remains difficult as well, even if there are some reasons for a possible improvement.

Figure 5: German motor vehicle production
![](images/d36b46fd241b19ba715acab2c44cfa0bb50f70a2aba01353bc4a38261ee1fb01.jpg)

## Final PMI confirms modest downside risk

This week’s final PMI for August saw a modest downward revision, largely due to services. At 51.0, it is consistent with growth at 1%ar on an ex-Ireland basis (Figure 6). But, this includes a lift from French services in August, which were boosted by the Paris Olympics. That effect should be temporary and suggests that the underlying signal from the PMI is a bit lower and hence also below our forecast, which has GDP growing at a 1%ar in the coming quarters. The shortfall is not large and therefore we stick to our forecast, but there are clear pressure points. One of these is manufacturing, the other is Germany.

The final inflation releases should come in line with preliminary estimates. The details will be important to monitor. We think Euro area services inflation was boosted by the Paris Olympics, a temporary effect in our view impacting hotel and transport prices. Controlling for the noise in our view leaves Euro area seasonally adjusted services prices up 3.5% ar over June-August, significantly less than the 5% ar pace seen earlier in the year.
