The ECB is almost certain to cut its deposit rate by another 25bp at next week’s meeting. A week later, the refinancing rate will also be cut by an additional 35bp, which reflects the new operating framework coming into force. But, money market rates are liked to the deposit rate and therefore its cut is the main policy lever. More details on the decision are in the Euro area essay.

The Euro area composite PMI was revised down marginally in the final report for August. But, that still leaves it up 0.8pts compared to July and signalling a 1.1%ar on GDP growth. However, French services were boosted by the Paris Olympics, which should be a temporary effect. Excluding this, the Euro area composite PMI is 1pt lower at around 50.0 and hence below the underlying momentum of around 1% we see in the coming quarters. The sense of downside risk is amplified by the persistent gap between manufacturing and services, and by the weakness in Germany.

Industrial orders rose 2.9%m/m in July, which puts them 24%ar above the 2Q24 level. Ex-bulk orders declined 0.4%m m, but this came after a 3%m/m gain in June. This leaves exbulk orders in July 3.4%ar above 2Q24 and they had already gained around 5%q/q saar in 2Q24. Hence, there looks to be some improvement. However, this is running far ahead of the signal from the manufacturing PMI, which has relapsed to just below 40 in August. By region, the signs of improvement are mostly in export orders, which have been stable for a while now, while domestic orders are still weaker.

Eurostat revised down the 2Q GDP estimate from 1.2% ar to 0.8% ar, while growth was revised up a little in 1Q from 1.1% ar to 1.3% ar. These numbers were dragged to some extent by Ireland, which can be volatile due to patents and the ownership of production facilities abroad. Excluding Ireland, the Euro area economy grew at a 1.2% ar average pace in 1H (1.4% in 1Q and 1.0% in 2Q), rather than 1.0%. The details showed weakness in domestic demand, both driven by the household and business sector.

German IP fell sharply in July, although the VDA data already point to a very strong rebound in car production in August. This suggests that holiday-related distortions are causing further swings in IP. In fact, the declines were very broad-based by product. Hence, while July looks very weak relative to 2Q24, it is far too early to draw conclusions about where IP will print in the current quarter. Some decline is likely however given the ongoing weakness of the business surveys.

The big caveat however is that German data are not available since April due to a technical issue at the German statistics office. Hence, the reliability of the current data is low. In terms of real incomes, there is a case for expecting Euro area consumer spending to pick up, but the data are struggling to validate this.

## Japan

• Incoming data suggest reflation is broadening

• July pay growth hits new high

• Corporate profit margins also at all-time highs in 2Q

• Next week: 2Q GDP revised up modestly; surveys mixed

A year and a half ago, we argued that Japan’s economy was on the cusp of entering a new reflationary phase, with wages, prices, and profits all poised to inflect higher. We wrote that Japan’s Great Reflation, as we put it, should see core inflation trend above the BoJ’s 2%oya target, wage growth accelerate toward 3%, and, as corporate price-setting behavior shifted, create a solid backdrop for profit growth.

As these forecasts have been borne out in the incoming data, we have also argued that the BoJ would embark on a gradual journey toward policy normalization. Importantly, we believed the BoJ’s path toward higher rates would be less sensitive to cyclical swings in Japan’s economy, and hinge more on a judgment that these reflationary dynamics were set to mark a sustained structural break with two decades of deflation.

Against this backdrop, we remain comfortable in forecasting another BoJ hike before year-end, and further rises in the pol icy rate through 2025. Indeed, on the heels of Ueda’s remarks last week, BoJ Board Member Takata reinforced the idea that BoJ is setting up for more hikes: he argued for the need to “shift gears” to create “a world with interest rates,” should inflation and wage dynamics evolve in line with expectations.

The past week’s data suggest that risks are now skewed toward more—not less—reflation. Constant-sample base pay growth for full-timers rose to 3%oya for the first time in the cycle; the more volatile data are also pointing to a sustained upswing in wage growth across the board. Amid strong results from this year’s Shunto negotiations, large minimum wage hikes, and continued labor market tightness, we think the risk is that momentum in wages accelerates beyond 3%. If so, real incomes will rise further, bolstering household spending. But persistent wage rises will likely also spell further stickiness in services-driven inflation.

Even as wage costs are rising, corporate profits are soaring. This week’s 2Q MoF survey suggested record profit margins both for manufacturers and non-manufacturers, reflecting solid gains in profit growth (Figure 1). The survey also implied a slight upward revision to capex data, which we expect will lift the second estimate of 2Q GDP next week a couple of tenths to 3.3%q/q saar, well above potential for Japan. Next

week will also bring the Reuters Tankan and Economy Watchers surveys, which we expect to show continued divergence between service sector resilience and more moderate manufacturing activity.

Figure 1: Japan base pay and corporate profits
![](images/cee512a98de0e56a3ddcba2951ab0e03cb168ff9f8928a6d7b1a6e1300d14418.jpg)

## July wage growth remains solid

Headline July wage data were almost in line with our forecast, with total earnings growth remaining elevated at 3.6%oya, following the 4.5%oya surge in June (Consensus: 2.9%; J.P. Morgan: 3.5%). The strong result reflected a continued rise in scheduled earnings growth, which is trending toward 3%, in line with our long-standing forecast and the results of the Shunto negotiations.

The most favorable news comes from robust base pay, with scheduled payments up a solid 2.7%oya in July, recording a three-decade high (Figure 2). Although part of the rise was due to changes in the part-time ratio caused by sample selection, constant sample data also suggest strong base pay growth. Total average scheduled earnings on a constant sample basis was up 4.8%oya in July. And growth in both fulland part-timers’ scheduled earnings continues to trend higher, up by two to three tenths to 3.0% and 2.8%oya, respectively. We expect momentum to continue to strengthen, potentially above the 3%oya pace suggested by the strong result of the Shunto wage negotiations.

Figure 2: Japan Total scheduled earnings
![](images/a9bacea8a005460e548da089756dc8708ebbf9baf4b35e9f4e8740c6d4ff4b39.jpg)

Volatile special payments, which include bonuses and other one-off payments, moderated to 6.2%oya, from an exceptionally strong 7.8%oya surge in June. Despite this modest decline in growth, still-elevated special payments are supporting solid total wage gains, keeping real wages positive for two consecutive months (Figure 3). We expect this dynamic to continue to support household consumption through 2H24.

Figure 6: Japan Nominal capex
Figure 3: Japan Composition of total cash earnings JPY thousands, both scales, sa
![](images/9ad9d2916f624159c07c23cf20f0632ca2f8fc1f255d8e78b757938a78c4e117.jpg)
Source: MHLW, J.P. Morgan. Seasonally adjusted by JPM.

Because historically high summertime bonuses boosted total earnings growth, real wage gains will be softer over the next few months as the influence from bonus payments fades (Figure 4). However, on a trend basis, continued rises in nominal base wages should leave wage growth momentum solid, supporting real wage growth to remain positive toward the end of the year.

Figure 4: Total and scheduled earnings, real %oya
![](images/2a7e44e7f95f84f5fcd568f7f75a7770f9a3a101549ea7634bd24901968abcc5.jpg)

## Record corporate margins in MoF survey

Details of the corporate survey were upbeat. Profits rose almost 30%q/q saar at the economy-wide level, reflecting double-digit sequential increases reported by manufacturers and other non-manufacturing sectors. These strong rises pushed our estimate of profit margins for manufacturers and non-manufacturers up to 9.4% and 7.3%, respectively, both clocking in at record highs on a seasonally adjusted basis (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Japan Ordinary profit margin
![](images/b659b4ed7eb84414ec1122dc77a0d23eaca4a1d22a84d9518da5b654fbb96043.jpg)

Our long-standing view has been that structural shifts in corporate-price setting behavior would allow for robust nominal gains in sales and profits, despite what has been a lackluster trend in real spending until recently. But the 2Q data suggest that corporates have nonetheless been able to expand margins, benefiting on net from currency depreciation and from a rebound in real private consumption last quarter.

The one wrinkle in the data concerns the split in activity between manufacturers and other sectors. While manufacturers reported a 10%q/q saar rebound in sales and strong profit growth, they trimmed capex outlays. Manufacturing-related capex fell 12.4%q/q saar in nominal terms, its second consecutive quarterly drop, even as non-manufacturers continued to increase equipment spending. This may reflect the impact of what has been a lackluster trend in Japan’s manufacturing and exports over the past year.

In this context, corporates reported a robust 9.1%oya rise in nominal capex (JPM: 8.0%oya; consensus: 8.3%). Excluding the impact of volatile software investment, the data look very close to the initial estimate of real capex in the 2Q GDP report. On our estimates, real capex is now tracking a 3.9%q/ q saar increase based on the MoF survey data, versus a 3.6% rise in the first GDP estimate (Figure 6).

![](images/833d22bfb77cb586b0d9a159457d548ea7be247fc636f80ee3c52dd5a91580f2.jpg)

## Canada

The Bank of Canada eased 25bp at its third consecutive meeting; we expect this pace to continue into 2025

While employment rebounded in August, the mix was unfavorable and the unemployment rate rose

• The July trade data point to a modest drag on GDP growth

Much like the Fed at its meeting in late July, this week the Bank of Canada had to make a policy decision just days before seeing the latest data on the labor market. Unlike the Fed, which did not ease then—and the subsequent Minutes suggest it might have gone a different way had that data been available at the meeting—the BoC adopted its third consecutive 25bp policy rate cut this week. The subsequent Labour Force Survey for August showed a recovery in employment growth, up 22.1K after two months of slight contraction. But the mix was unfavorable as full-time employment plunged over 43K while part-time employment surged by nearly 66K.

Moreover, the unemployment rate rose another two-tenths to 6.6%, which (outside of the pandemic) is a level last seen over seven years ago. Part of the rise in unemployment reflects increased labor supply due to entry by migrants and young workers, but there are recent signs of some deterioration in the participation rate by prime-aged workers (25-54 year-olds) while young workers (16-24 year-olds) have seen their participation rate drop more precipitously and steadily since 2022 (Figure 1). This softening on the supply side of the labor market ought to be a concern for the BoC, even as they reiterated the view this week that “excess supply” will put downward pressure over time on still-elevated wage growth.

Figure 1: Labor force participation rates by age group
![](images/3950c9be151f76abae5a64c21b2e1e679dc1990cc2fc7c3b236b7b82b31d18ae.jpg)

These participation moves coincide with other signs of fading momentum in employment: in addition to the large decline in full-time employment in August mentioned above, the strong employment support that had been coming from the service sector since the pandemic recovery also has started to fade. Job growth remains relatively solid, but the BoC continues to be concerned that labor market slack could grow and become

a drag on the expansion.

Meanwhile, Governor Macklem provided further dovish guidance, suggesting that if inflation continues to decline broadly in line with the Bank’s forecast, “it is reasonable to expect further cuts in our policy rate.” We expect the BoC to continue on a 25bp-per-meeting easing pace at least through 1Q25, and then cut once more during 2Q25 to bring the policy rate down to 3% by midyear. That said, we continue to see risks skewed toward a more accelerated and ultimately deeper easing cycle. Further signs of a loss of momentum in the US labor market this week keep us looking for a 50bp cut by the Fed in September. Macklem repeated that he did not think the policy divergence between the BoC and Fed was a relevant consideration for the former’s policy stance, but we believe that could change if a more rapid pace of Fed easing is realized.

## Trade remains a drag

The July merchandise trade data released this week point to a continued modest drag of trade on 2Q GDP growth tracking, even as the nominal trade deficit swung from a \$179mn deficit in June to a surplus of \$684mn in July. As StatsCan noted alongside the release, such swings are not that unusual. In real (constant dollar) terms, the trade deficit narrowed modestly, from \$4.4bn to \$4.2bn in July. Both imports and exports declined on the month: down 1.7 and 0.4%, respectively in nominal terms, and declining 1.9 and 1.5%, respectively, in real terms (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Real merchandise trade volumes
![](images/84cce1d8814ac326bddebe2d1328e846d75a28001fddce712e8f10454e657632.jpg)

In the details, a sharp drop in real imports of autos and of machinery and equipment led the July decline and suggests weakening demand for durable goods and for capital equipment. Real exports also declined in these categories but were more broadly soft, with energy and chemical exports also declining on the month. The short-lived railroad lockdown last month might contribute to some further (but temporary) softness in trade volumes next month. Meanwhile, the recent appreciation of the real effective exchange rate for Canada— despite the BoC easing ahead of a number of its main trading partners—should further weigh on exports.

## Mexico

• Domestic demand data mixed but losing steam

• Economic sentiment and PMIs on a similar note

• August Inflation and July IP next week in focus

• Judiciary Reform to be passed in the Senate next week

This week we got further confirmation of the cross-currents taking place in the domestic economy. There is still some momentum on consumption and some qualitative indicators that point in the direction of trend-like growth. This could even result in a modest upgrade to growth in 3Q. However, dusting off the specs of optimism, business outlook and investment figures are aligning with a more bearish story, but probably from 4Q onward.

June domestic demand data were mixed, as was the case through the first half of the year. Private consumption was up 0.1%m/m whereas gross fixed investment came in at -1%. The better yield in June consumption followed a very weak rest of 2Q, whereas GFI had been on an up-trend for most of the year after falling in 2H23. Amid all these twists and turns some things appear certain: looking back, domestic demand lost steam, particularly investment; private consumption has moderated, but still expanded at a decent 3% annualized pace in 1H24 vs December. Putting numbers to 2Q24, private consumption was down 2.5% annualized, the first quarterly drop since 2020, mostly driven by a plunge in imported goods consumption.

![](images/d9061d4a3c579e983261f906e59383030a6d90d2949348edcd5892a3becb68ed.jpg)

Uncertainty has grown and will likely weigh on investment starting in late 3Q, but private consumption should remain well supported by fundamentals. Data point in that direction, particularly early in 3Q: social security transfers surged in July after being suspended during elections (Mar-Jun), real wage income continues to rapidly grow, credit is expanding at a solid clip, and available consumption indicators such as auto sales and card spending showed a big jump in the month. Furthermore, survey data for retail sales and services demand were robust through August. The IMEF services PMI also

held up at a healthy level, with both output and new orders (Figure 1) edging slightly lower but staying firmly above the expansion threshold (50).

Consumption should be a tailwind for growth this quarter and should be complemented by external demand, which is seemingly finally picking up on a more sustainable basis. To this point, manufacturing exports surged in July (6.1%m/m, sa) and the composition of growth is broader based than in past quarters, when it was heavily skewed toward auto-related exports. The August PMI data were mixed. Manufacturing sentiment was strong, as were indicators of production and domestic and export demand reported in separate surveys by INEGI. By contrast, the IMEF PMI maintained the downbeat signal it has been sending for much of the past two years.

That said, all manufacturing survey data were weak in July, leading us to expect a decline in manufacturing output in next week’s IP data for the month (-0.6%m/m, sa), even amid massive export growth, but this comes after a 2% gain in June and should be followed by another gain in August, as per the filtering of information currently at hand. The risks to our 1%ar 3Q GDP growth forecast remain skewed to the upside.

On the other hand, the picture for investment is souring: business sentiment was still positive through August, and capex plans in fact ticked up, but this came before the big shock of uncertainty brought about by the political transition in Mexico and the discussion of the judicial reform, which should be approved soon, and has led to the market re-pricing higher Mexico’s country risk, something we expect to be at least partly manifest in investment spending decisions. It likely will take some time to see the impact on hard data, but survey data as soon as September (out October) should show this rise in risk perception (Figure 2) already visible in faster-adjusting financial markets.

Figure 2: Business environment outlook
![](images/f34d5dd0f52ba5c317941f2d9319cd097943b71e815ae355eb67129e30e9dbe0.jpg)

## CPI: No wolf at the door, but the driveway?

We expect next week’s CPI data to cement the reversal in headline inflation toward lower rates after a brief spike driven by non-core inflation, particularly food prices. We expect prices to have risen 0.1% in the month and 5.1% in annual terms (from 5.6% in July). Food prices, on the margin, likely perked up again, however, in the second half of the month on meat and egg prices, but not dramatically so. Furthermore, global energy prices falling recently, if sustained, could bring some relief to liquefied gas prices and more modestly to gasoline that would help offset this recent re-acceleration in some food items.

Away from the volatile components, the short-term picture remains benign. We expect no meaningful impact of currency depreciation just yet, and, in fact, core goods price gains should remain well contained. In turn, services inflation is also cooling on the margin as domestic demand took a breather last quarter after an impressive run in earlier years. Private consumption contracted for the first time since 2020 in 2Q. We expect the benign trend in core inflation to be short-lived (Figure 3), and we look for sequential price pressures to build as we move into next year as a combination of currency weakness, consumption resilience after its brief breather, and weak investment to prove an inflation-prone mix. In annual terms we continue to expect core at 3.7% this year. Our seasonally-adjusted path still shows 3m/3m annualized core running below 4%.

Figure 3: Core Goods CPI
![](images/e5d77770b4f761450b526497c3fe1bce806732243322be16d3ee04c63a32c109.jpg)

## Reforms Update

After the Lower House fast-tracked the Judiciary Reform in Congress, now it is time for the Upper House (i.e., the Senate) to move forward with the bill. The Constitutional Issues Commission was summoned to meet on Thursday to vote the Dictamen of the Reform. Note the Bill was not voted by the same commission in the Lower House as its discussion by the Permanent Commission in August freed Morena from doing so again. This was feasible because the House was the one that prepared the Dictamen.

Once the Commission approves the bill on Sunday, it will be turned to the full Senate for the general vote (Tuesday and Wednesday). The Morena coalition does not have a Constitutional majority in the Senate, as it is short by one vote. While we do not expect the opposition to close ranks completely, all eyes are on the slim chances for this to happen. If the Senate approves the bill, then it will be turned to the Local Chambers, which only need a simple majority approval in at least 17 States. If it is not approved, it will be returned to the Lower House for amendments.

## Brazil

• GDP expanded beyond our above consensus expectations in 1H24

The expansion is driven by strength in domestic demand, in contrast to agriculture-led growth in 2023

• A reversal in inventories ahead and data for 3Q suggest GDP growth moderating

Next week: we see services rising 0.3%, headline retail sales falling 1% and the IBC-Br down 0.7% in July

While the 2Q GDP report confirmed our view that economic growth would rebound in the first half of the year, it did so to levels far beyond our initial predictions. At the beginning of the year, we were anticipating 1H24 GDP to expand around 2.5%q/q saar on average, coming out of close to zero in 2H23, but the economy grew a whopping 5% in the last semester. What’s more, the driver of such expansion was domestic demand. In contrast last year’s 1H23 growth of 3.5% that was driven by the agricultural sector and a rise of external demand, 1H24 growth was a result of an average of 7.6% expansion in domestic final sales while external demand dropped 9% due to higher imports that are fulfilling the needs of such internal strength (Figure 1). Such strength in the domestic economy over two sequential quarters was last seen in 2010, if we ignore the pandemic economic growth swings.

Figure 1: GDP growth and domestic final sales
![](images/93ffe35bde4398a9175f9e517b2df09c5a8abb0fb165f5d7efe07b3b13e428ce.jpg)

This week’s stronger-than-expected 2Q GDP growth led us to upgrade our GDP forecast from 2.9% to 3.2%y/y, indicating that this year’s growth will be stronger than last year’s. In this forecast, we assume a moderation of economic growth going forward. Even if 2Q GDP was quite solid across the board, it was also partly explained by a jump in inventories that is unlikely to be repeated in the next quarters, probably helping to slow GDP growth rates.

Fundamentally, the effect of the public stimulus is close to being over and for the first time in a while both fiscal and monetary policy will act to curb economic activity in an environment of already compressed corporate margins, falling profitability and rising costs. While this is growth-negative, other parts of the fundamentals indicate continued solid GDP growth. First, personal savings have moved up in 2Q, despite the strong expansion in consumption, and this spare savings can be used in next quarters. Second, banking leverage remains low and could support loan growth further in the future.

The initial data for 3Q suggest that the expansion is bending but is far from breaking. After a 4.3% jump in June, the industrial sector seems to be adapting to the rise of inventories and industrial production decreased 1.4%m/m sa in July, reverting some of the gains in 2Q. But amid high capacity utilization, investments in construction and capital goods have been rising since the beginning of the year, with the exception of May when floods hit Brazil’s southernmost state (Figure 2). This leaves some good prospects for growth in 3Q.

Figure 2: Investment growth and capacity utilization
![](images/9088898252af5caae7048861b5243a2616133d4430aa764356ba5f214f3c6274.jpg)

Sentiment indicators for August were equally softer than in previous months but again only moderating from very high levels. The composite PMI went down from 56.0 in July to 52.9 in August, marking the lowest level since the beginning of the year. This deterioration was mostly driven by a fall 4-point fall in the manufacturing PMI to 50.4, but the services PMI also moderated about 2-points to 54.2 (Figure 3). If in one side the new orders for manufacturing fell markedly, the employment metrics of both PMIs remain at solid levels.

Figure 3: Manufacturing and services PMI
![](images/d3033aef43f6382bbb964b5288b39afd6dc5477150a0c70659b9b18fb3bd535d.jpg)

Also, in August vehicle production declined 1.8%m/m sa after two months of outstanding growth when production reached close to the post-pandemic highs, whereas vehicle sales decreased 3.4% and remains at relatively low levels (Figure 4). Those results are consistent with production at double digit pace while vehicle sales would contract in 3Q.

Figure 4: Vehicle production and sales
![](images/5d5a28e4230d46e219af33ee0a412294e8234a8b72191a01bc84761b6a6f4aa6.jpg)
Source: Anfavea and J.P. Morgan

We anticipate the same trend of moderation in next week’s releases. We look for services to grow 0.3%m/m sa, a rather solid pace but well below the 1.7% jump registered in June. Headline sales should fall 1% though core sales should grow after a sharp decline in June. The economic activity index should fall 0.7%m/m sa in July, leaving a carry-over (i.e. if the indicator is stable in Aug and Sep) of about 1%-1.5%q/q saar for 3Q, closer to our call for a 2% expansion in 3Q but well below the nowcaster that is around 5% (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Brazil 3Q nowcaster
![](images/9d0fc8a56360e56ea592187f1e91fc9ed6fa239ea11891d6a43a6324562e5882.jpg)

Political focus will turn over how to offset the negative effect of the payroll tax break and over the 4<sup>th</sup> fiscal review, whose deadline is Sep 22. Treasury Secretary Ceron did not rule out blocking expenses, rather highlighting that revenues performed well in August and dismissing the idea of changing the fiscal target (‘Mudança na meta fiscal não está em debate no governo, afirma Rogério Ceron,’ Valor, Sep 5). Still, given the negative reaction to the cooking gas aid proposal, we see his comments as less consequential than the Finance Minister’s vow to review the aid terms. In our view Haddad’s

promise is worth following through to avoid the perception that the government is creating holes on the fiscal framework.

## Argentina

The cash-basis current account posted a US\$1.6bn deficit in July, the second in a row

• The deterioration was driven by higher imports payments and a broadening in the services deficit

• By our estimates, net FX reserves stood at -US\$5.3bn (as of August 23) compared to -US\$8.6bn 2023eoy

• Net and liquid reserves are projected to recover in 4Q, conditional on policy decisions

The Milei administration has signaled that the top priority at this point is accelerating the disinflation speed over all other policy objectives, including reserves accumulation. Against this backdrop, we take stock on reserves accumulation in the last months and what we expect through year-end.

Meanwhile, the fiscal consolidations remains the key anchor of the economic program. We expect “PAIS tax” collection revenue sacrifice of around 0.3% of GDP through year-end after the administration reduction of the tax rate on goods imports and freight services by 10%-pts effective in September. But we also estimate that the approval of the fiscal package in Congress could add up to 0.7%-pt of GDP in additional fiscal revenues this year, offsetting the “PAIS tax” rate cut. Ahead, we continue to emphasize that a (sustainable) rebound in economic activity becomes a necessary condition to support tax collection.

## July cash-basis current account in red

The cash-basis current account recorded a US\$1.6bn deficit in July, following a US\$0.2bn deficit in June. The July deterioration was primarily driven by the US\$1.6bn outflow related to interest payments to bondholders. Additionally, both the merchandise trade balance and the services shortfall continued to worsen over the month (see note for details).

The year-to-date cash-basis current account posted a substantial US\$7.3bn surplus, a significant turnaround from the US\$6.4bn deficit recorded during the same period last year. This reversal is primarily attributed to a US\$14.2bn decline in import payments, while export inflows saw a US\$1.7bn reduction. The underperformance in exports can be explained by the Blend FX arrangement, under which 20% of total exports (US\$9.5 billion YTD) are supplied to the financial FX market. On the imports side, the YTD decline is due to both import payment restrictions and a downturn in economic activity (see note). Notably, the BCRA has relaxed import payment regulations at the official FX rate. Since August, import payments can be made in two installments (down from four previously), further normalizing the import payment chain. Note the ratio of cash versus accrued imports reached a

new high of 100% in July.

The financing account recorded a US\$1bn shortfall in July, primarily due to amortizations of hard-currency bonds (US\$1.3bn) and intervention in the parallel FX market (US\$326mn on the month, so US\$14.8mn/day on average). This adds to about US\$400mn deployed in August, by our estimates. In all, the current and the financial accounts drove gross reserves lower by US\$2.6bn in July, the largest drag observed since October 2023.

## Net reserves flat in August...

Despite the intervention (formally sterilization) in the the parallel FX, the BCRA was able to purchase US\$535mn in August at the official FX market, following US\$184mn in sales between June and July (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Reserves accumulation and FX gap
![](images/d28bfe763fa4db635c47cb4481fc957bf5b532712acc3f1da736de4bb4f4ffda.jpg)

Regarding net FX reserves, we estimate they stood at -US\$5.3bn (as of August 23), relatively flat from the prior month yet up from -US\$8.6bn by December 23 (our estimate considers as hard-currency liabilities Bopreal series 2 amortizations for around US\$2bn in the next 12 months but not Treasury deposits). Meanwhile, USD liquid reserves (when excluding China swap line, BIS, and gold) stood at around US\$4.5bn. It is worth noting that the Bank’s USD reserves requirements have increased by US\$2.3bn since the end of June, in part explained by the US\$1.3bn increase in USD deposits amid the new tax amnesty launched in August.

## … but are expected to recover in 4Q

We expect net reserves to remain under pressure through the end of 3Q24 and recover in 4Q24, depending on policy decisions. Starting with the drags, the aforementioned BCRA import payment schedule change starting in September will imply higher import payments ahead, the flip side of which will be easing USD import demand at the blue-chip swap market. The reduction of the PAIS tax on goods imports and freight services by 10%-pts could also trigger higher import demand at the official FX. Regarding external debt service, we assume multilaterals (ex-IMF) debt service is fully

Week of September 9 - 13

financed through year-end. We also entertain US\$0.8bn in parallel FX intervention through end-2024, and similar inflows into the parallel FX market due to the tax amnesty/ RIGI (which should allow for the lifting of the 80/20 export blend scheme close to year-end).

Under these main assumptions, net reserves are to decline by around US\$2bn through October-end and recover by US\$2.7bn in the last two months of the year (assuming the Blend FX is eliminated). This is consistent with net reserves closing the year at around -US\$4.5bn and liquid reserves (excluding China swap line, BIS, and gold) close to US\$5bn. Note that this is a conservative estimate as we are not accounting for potential increases in liquid reserves associated with higher USD deposits amid the tax amnesty, repos, or other loans.

## Fiscal package to compensate “PAIS tax”

August real tax collection landed back in negative territory after a slight rebound in July. Total real tax collection (J.P. Morgan’s seasonal adjustment) dropped by -5.2%m/m, sa, from +3.2% m/m reported the previous month. The bulk of the monthly contraction in real tax collection was driven by a decline in foreign trade-related taxes and “PAIS tax” collection, down by -18.7% and -6.1%m/m, sa, respectively. On an over-year-ago basis, real tax revenues declined by 13.7%oya in August (when deflating by trailing last 12-m inflation), making it a -7.8% contraction YTD (Figure 2, see note for more details).

Figure 2: Total tax revenues
![](images/564b511edf6cdff9a7b5149fda54ed589c06c6be158325b34dafa420a2487c0b.jpg)

Ahead, we expect “PAIS tax” collection to decrease after the administration confirmed the reduction of the tax rate on goods imports and freight services by 10%-pts, effective September 2, and its full elimination by December. We estimate that reduction could result in 0.3%-pt of GDP in tax revenue sacrifice through year-end. But we also estimate that the approval of the fiscal package in Congress could add up to 0.7%-pt of GDP in additional fiscal revenues this year, more than offsetting the “PAIS tax” reduction drag over revenues.

That said, we watch for the fate of the pension bill, which was recently vetoed by the president but if overturned by Congress could result in additional fiscal drag of 0.4% pf GDP this year and 1% next.

Given the temporary nature of most of the taxes approved under the fiscal package, we continue to emphasize that a (sustainable) rebound in economic activity becomes a necessary condition to support tax collection ahead. We estimate that the elimination of the “PAIS” tax by December could imply around 1.3% of GDP in less revenues next year, while a more appreciated REER in 2025 (when compared to 2024) could imply a drag of around 0.3% of GDP in exports withholding taxes, all else equal. Finally, the one-off increase of the income tax on the back of year-end 2023 FX devaluation, for around 1%-pt of GDP, is likely not to be repeated next year. In all, we estimate a drag of around 2.5% of GDP in tax revenues for next year, which should be offset in part by the fiscal package approved in Congress, but mostly by an economic activity rebound boosting tax revenues.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: The Board unanimously decided to lower the monetary policy interest rate by 25bp, to 5.5%

The Board also signaled a faster convergence of the monetary policy rate toward its neutral level

• We had expected a more cautious approach, considering that faster easing could risk FX pass-through

We now foresee two more 25bp cuts this year, so for the year-end policy rate to close at 5.0%

• We maintain 4.5% as the terminal rate, based on the premise that r\* is at the ceiling of the range

The Board unanimously decided to lower the monetary policy interest rate by 25bp to 5.5%. This decision surprised us (we had a non-consensus call) as we had anticipated that the Board would maintain the rate steady, given the current inflation levels and the year-to-date performance of economic activity, particularly after incorporating July's data. However, it appears that the Board's focus has shifted, now placing significant weight on the expected behavior of the U.S. Federal Reserve. This shift, in our view, likely explains today’s rate cut as well as a change in the forward guidance, signaling the reduction of the monetary policy rate toward its neutral level may occur more rapidly than previously anticipated in June (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile nominal and real monetary policy rate %, deflating nominal MPR with 12-m ahead inflation as per EEF
![](images/b4a695ede59d41110658db4298d79e4212f11765ec271a4843016988f446d61f.jpg)
Source: INE, CBC and JPMorgan

Regarding economic activity, the Board’s discussion centered on the second quarter, largely overlooking the latest activity print. The statement acknowledges that activity lost momentum during the second quarter, with a larger than expected decline, while July’s Imacec showed a significant acceleration in month-on-month activity. However, the Board tempers this signal by noting that “as in previous quarters, a relevant portion of this increase was due to one-off factors, in a context of significant volatility in monthly figures.” While we recognize the volatility in seasonally and calendar-adjusted series, both headline and ex-mining activity have been running at a YTD annualized pace above potential through July (see note). That said, we agree that private consumption in the second quarter was slightly weaker than expected (see note) and that unemployment, when adjusted for seasonality, has edged higher (see note).

The Board’s discussion on inflation was succinct. The statement mentions that headline inflation, currently at 4.4% year over year, was “somewhat higher than forecasted in their central scenario." However, the Board focused on core CPI, running at 3.4%, and anchored inflation expectations over the two-year policy horizon as justification for the 25bp rate cut. Notably absent is any acknowledgment of the fact that 12- month inflation expectations have risen by 60 basis points over the past two months, to 3.8% (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Chile inflation expectations %-pt deviation from 3% target
![](images/902645032e4a8ce62bde0c9a047b9b5a660536f30622b58ddeda72da04f4a5c8.jpg)
Source: CBC and J.P. Morgan

The Board concludes that the combined information suggests modest changes in the outlook for economic activity, with spending showing greater weakness. This, coupled with twoyear inflation expectations at 3%, reduces the risks of further inflationary persistence in the medium term due to the “cost shock”—likely referring to the electricity price adjustment, although this is not explicitly mentioned in the statement. Against this backdrop, the Board highlights that the reduction of the policy rate toward its neutral level is likely to proceed more quickly than anticipated in June.

We had expected a more cautious approach, considering that faster easing could risk adding FX pass-through to the electricity price adjustment shock in an economy where we don’t observe slack as measured by GDP (though we do recognize slack in the labor market). However, the Board seems to have de-prioritized these risks, likely focusing on the evolution of the interest rate differential with the Fed as it begins its easing cycle. Beyond the cyclical discussion, we stress that the quarterly forward guidance has proven too volatile, causing the central bank’s reaction function to vary practically on a quarterly basis.

On the macro central scenario, the midpoint of the 2024 GDP growth range was revised a bit lower as the range now goes from 2.25-2.75% (J.P. Morgan 2.6%y/y). Despite the mild correction to the annual GDP growth, the output gap for 2024 suffered an important revision, difficult to rationalize, in our view. The 2H24 output gap was revised to -0.6% from +0.4%,

CPI

## Peru

a whole point. For the calendar year as a whole, the output gap is now estimated at -0.1%, which compares to +0.5% before. This major correction stemming from the estimation of a latent variable seems to be the main innovation in the central scenario, allowing for the dovish change in forward guidance. By the way, the Staff revised the 2024-2033 trend GDP (ex.mining) growth rate lower to 1.8%, from 1.9% previously. As we stressed in a recent note, YTD the annualized growth pace of ex. mining activity runs at a 3.7%ar pace (see note). This point was downplayed in the report.

As relevant, though in theory operating on the opposite side, Dec’24 headline was revised higher by 20bp to 4.5%oya (J.P.Morgan 4.46%). In a similar vein, Dec’24 core CPI was revised by +10bp to 3.9%oya (J.P.Morgan 4.0%). While 2026 inflation was revised slightly lower, the revision higher of the year-end headline inflation makes it challenging to rationalize the change in the Board’s guidance for a faster convergence to neutral levels on the evolution of the domestic variables.

Finally, on the forward guidance itself, the Board is signaling the midpoint of the corridor to converge to 5.25% by 4Q24 and 4.2% by 4Q25 (quarterly averages), and it maintains 4.0% as its neutral nominal policy rate. Assuming the Board maintains its forward guidance in this last quarter of the year as the Fed eases monetary conditions, we now foresee two more 25 basis point cuts this year. We thus revise the year-end policy rate to 5.0% and maintain 4.5% as the terminal rate, based on the premise that r\* is at the ceiling of the CBC’s estimated range (3.5-4.5%, with the CBC resorting to the midpoint as point estimate).

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

DMP moderates but still shows wage and price expectations hovering too high

• Further speculation Reeves could change the fiscal rules

We expect next week’s labour market and GDP data to keep the BoE on track for cautious easing

This week was again light on data. While we await next week’s installment of official labour market data, it was noteworthy that the BoE’s DMP survey this week showed yearahead wage expectations holding at 4.1%. More generally the DMP clearly flags moderating inflation pressures, but the level across some readings is still a concern. The BoE, for example, expects private sector wage growth to be running close to 3% next year and the DMP - which unlike the REC has broadly tracked the official data - continues to flag some upside risk (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Forward looking surveys of wage growth
% bal., sa, permanent salaries %oya, 3mma, ex. bonuses, private sector, AWE
![](images/f9930dec3967a05a40c2685a4e67369c6beefcbd6f9b3c42f2cf86b76717b6a1.jpg)
Source: ONS, KPMG, REC, BoE

Next week we expect the official data on private sector regular average earnings to show a 0.4%m/m gain, which would leave the three-month annualised change at 4.2%. It’s a similar story for business’ CPI expectations in the DMP, which have fallen a lot from the peak but more recently have stabilised at levels a little above the inflation target (Figure 2).

Figure 2: DMP business price expectations
![](images/6cf98f5c9651a6e3b0aa31db6c0de92578ec95ac50b288de2afc1eb8340009c1.jpg)
Recent strength could start to produce more pressure on the labour market and inflation. The picture is still unclear, and

our current forecast does still anticipate a slowing in growth to 1.5% annualised in 3Q. But an upside surprise in July GDP next week, relative to our 0.1%m/m forecast, would further highlight the issue - especially if met with a firm print on employment or wages from the labour market report.

Speculation on the next Budget continued this week. The focus shifted a little away from tax increases and more towards the prospect of a potential change in the fiscal rules. As Labour develops a bill on fiscal responsibility, stipulating the OBR should be involved when large decisions are made on tax and spending, it rejected a Conservative amendment stating that changes to the fiscal rules should also be included in OBR evaluations. We think this is more about political point scoring for the opposition, as no government would probably want to tie its hands on the precise definition of debt used in the current fiscal rules (net debt excluding the BoE’s balance sheet) or create obstacles to small changes. The bill Labour is pushing though is more about preventing the kind of large and sudden policy changes implemented by the 2022 mini-Budget.

Labour has already spoken about reviewing the fiscal rules to consider the use of a target for net worth. The issue is live and supports our view that Labour will find it hard to resist sticking to a rule that includes BoE APF flows. Previously we have estimated that it could free up £17bn of headroom by removing those flows, with a further £10-15bn coming from the window on the five year debt target naturally rolling forward by an additional year.

## Sweden and Norway

• We expect a downside surprise for many in August Norway CPI

• In both countries, data still point to modest, below potential growth...

• ...but risks on the Swedish labor market are increasing

Recent Swedish data on activity have been mixed. The 2Q GDP number was revised up from -3.2% to -1.0%q/q, saar, marking the eighth consecutive quarter with GDP switching between growth and contraction. Through the noise, GDP has now been roughly unchanged over the past three years. Since the Riksbank started raising rates private domestic final sales have clearly underperformed, driven by household consumption (also the case in 2Q) as high interest rate sensitivity and depressed real disposable incomes have weighed on activity. But, we think more sustainable (albeit still modest) growth is underway. With inflation coming down and the Riksbank having started its easing cycle, real disposable incomes should improve and underpin consumption and the housing market.

From the business side, the manufacturing PMI rebounded strongly in August which more than offset a fall in the services PMI, leading to a higher composite PMI. We think the manufacturing index could edge slightly higher while services should stay close to current levels. There were small changes in the August NIER survey, with the headline index therefore still supportive of our view that growth will modestly rebound 1.0%q/q, saar, in 3Q . We see some upside risk from inventory building and improving real disposable incomes, but somewhat worrisome signals from various labor market indicators temper these upside risks (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Sweden labor market indicators
![](images/0565ba7a8b0637a74a78a63ca46b6d1d4d1267fdd6e56f5b6a22d2fa010d041c.jpg)

Looking ahead, we expect core inflation at 2.1%oya in August (2.15% on a second decimal) while CPIF inflation should fall to 1.3%oya. These would be the lowest readings since December 2021 and December 2020, respectively. With inflation risks subsiding focus for the Riksbank is turning

more to the real economy and the labor market—and whether these could trigger a 50bps cut (as discussed in the August minutes). We do, however, not think upcoming data will warrant such a move. We expect the LFS unemployment rate to decline slightly to 8.5% while the July GDP indicator should only fall 0.2%m/m sa after a strong outcome in June.

## Norway: Prepare for a big CPI surprise

There have been few data releases in recent weeks—and no change to the overall picture of modest, below potential growth. After ticking up one tenth to 2.1% the registered unemployment rate reversed back to 2.0% in August. House prices rebounded 0.5%m/m, sa, in August, suggesting still solid demand—even if recent months’ outcome have been slightly below Norges Bank’s projection. Core retail sales also rebounded 0.9%m/m in July after significant volatility in prior months . Through the noise, retail sales have been stagnating over the past year. Household goods consumption, which is directly included GDP, fell 4.3%m/m (owing to car purchases). Comparing both retail sales and good consumption to the 2Q average, they point to private consumption contracting in 3Q.

There are two important releases ahead of the September Norges Bank meeting. First, we think the output indicators in the 3Q Regional Network Survey will marginally decline, somewhat conflicting with the (volatile) manufacturing PMI. We also envisage employment and wage expectations for next year to ease slightly, in line with signals from the Expectations Survey. Second, we project a big downside surprise in August core inflation at 2.9%oya (both vs. consensus and Norges Bank). This is caused by price changes for kindergartens, which according to our estimate will lower core inflation by 0.36%-pt in isolation. As usual, food prices are a big uncertainty. Prices have been clearly higher than suggested by usual indicators, but there is downside risk on the back of fines for the three largest supermarkets, which constitute 95% of the Norwegian grocery market, for illegally collaborating on price adjustments. This could incentivize lower prices near-term.

Figure 2: Norges Bank core inflation forecast history

## Emerging Europe

• Türkiye: August CPI inflation shows strong momentum, 2Q24 sees weak growth

## • Fiscal policy set to tighten markedly in 2025

Türkiye’s headline CPI rose 2.5% m/m nsa in August, above expectations. Headline inflation fell sharply to 52.0% oya in August, from 61.8% in July, on the back of strong favorable base effects (Figure 1). The slight upside surprise to our forecast mainly stemmed from transportation and clothing prices.

Services prices rose by a high 4.6% m/m with broad-based price hikes across sectors. Core goods prices rose 1.2% m/m in August, up from 0.4% in July leading to a over-year-ago inflation rate to 28.9% oya in August, from 38.3% oya in July. Core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose 3.0% m/m in August (versus 2.5% m/m in July), leading oya core inflation down to 51.6% in August, versus 60.2% in July. Food prices fell 1.1 % m/m (44.9% oya in August from 58.9% in July). Energy prices rose 6.8% m/m due to the natural gas price hike of 27.6% for households nationwide.

![](images/09a55b61658c30ba19a148b96d85ca2e86774a22b952afadb5599f69219e1493.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

Seasonally-adjusted core inflation (C index) increased to 2.9% m/m in August, from 2.5% in July, due to both services and core goods prices. The latter were boosted by Turkish lira depreciation in August. Core inflation momentum (C index 3m change, saar, %) fell to 32.7% in August, from 34.2% July. We still expect core inflation momentum to slow owing to core goods in 2H24 thanks to real lira appreciation and the slowdown in economic activity.

We expect inflation above 2% m/m nsa in September due to prices gains for rent, transportation and education services in the month. We maintain our end-2024 inflation forecast at 42.5% oya and end-2025 inflation forecast at 25% oya (Figure 2). We believe that the risks around inflation forecasts are to the downside as the fiscal stance turns contractionary in the coming months (see Table 1 below).

Figure 2: Türkiye inflation forecast
![](images/3469b777b94a792838437565ca3320ebcab4da727993b0457a7428598c0b7cd2.jpg)

## Türkiye sees weak 2Q24 growth

GDP growth slowed sharply to 0.1% q/q sa in 2Q24, from 1.4% in 1Q24 (sharply revised down from 2.4%). The slowdown was driven by all the expenditure components excluding inventories. The contribution of private consumption to q/ q sa growth fell to 0.3%-pt in 2Q24 (from 0.5%-pt in 1Q24), government consumption fell to 0%-pt (from +0.6%-pt in 1Q24), and the contribution of investments declined to -1.0%-pt (from +0.6%-pt, Figure 3). In over year ago terms, 2Q24 GDP growth came in 2.5% versus 5.3% oya in 1Q24 (revised down from 5.7%), below expectations.

![](images/ae359db7cce6ea23267d3a541ee335ee8fc761b1828f2dad86b401ee82bb7332.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

On the back of tight monetary and fiscal policies, sequential quarterly contractions in GDP are likely in 2H24. Loan growth remained subdued in July and August, suggesting private consumption remains soft. The real sector confidence indices also confirm the slowdown in activity. We maintain our GDP growth forecast of 3.0% this year, which implies declines in GDP over 2H24. And we revise down our 2025 GDP growth forecast to 3% (previously 3.5%) as leading indicators signal weakening momentum, while fiscal policy is likely to turn contractionary in 2025.

In the medium-term economic programme (MTP) announced this week, the central government budget deficit targets are revised down to 4.9% of GDP in 2024 (previously 6.4% of GDP) and 3.1% of GDP (previously 3.4% of GDP) in 2025, down from 5.2% of GDP in 2023. The GDP growth target is revised down to 3.5% oya in 2024 (previously 4.0%) and 4.0% in 2025 (previously 4.5%), down from 5.1% in 2023.

Poland:

MTP forecast the unemployment rate to increase marginally from 9.3% in 2024 to 9.6% in 2025. CPI inflation is forecast to drop to 41.5% at end-24 and 17.5% at end-25, down from 64.8% in 2023 (Table 1). In our view the the inflation targets are rather ambitious given the GDP growth target of 3.5% (JPMe: 3%) for 2024 and 4% (JPMe: 3%) for 2025. But fiscal tightening is likely to support disinflation. We still expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate by 250bp each in November and December to 45% by year-end. There are downside risks to our forecast for the end-25 policy rate of 30% if the fiscal tightening projected in the medium-term program is delivered.

We expect headline CPI to drop sharply to 4.9% from 5.4% in July, but core inflation to move in the opposite direction. On core, while momentum remains relatively soft and in line with prior recent months, the base effect does not help. As for non-core drivers, food inflation is pushing inflation higher also on base effects - the monthly pace is expected to be negative. The biggest push for disinflation comes from fuel prices which likely dropped both in m/m and oya terms.

We think the CBR will keep the policy rate unchanged at 18.0%, but risks are skewed toward another rate hike. Signs are growing that domestic demand growth is gradually losing momentum, RUB has remained relatively well behaved. The inflation picture apparently has not worsened since the last meeting, but the August CPI report may change this perception and tip the balance toward a hike in case of a meaningful upside surprise.

## South Africa

• Growth momentum bounced in 2Q as purchasing power squeeze faded

• 4Q growth uplift hinges on pension reform impact, with moderate downside risk to 1%y/y projection

• Current account deficit eased to 0.9% in 2Q

After a flat first quarter economic output rose 0.4%q/q sa in 2Q24 (1.8%q/q saar), in line with our projection. The main support came from household consumption which was up 1.4%q/q sa (5.8% saar), contributing 0.9%pt to GDP growth (Table 1). We think this was on the back of an easing in the purchasing power drag combined with nominal wage gains. Semi-durables and services spending benefited from the uptick, paring back losses in 1Q.

However, fixed investment spending disappointed as private as well as the public sector spending contracted. Most segments of investment spending saw declines with further steep contractions in residential and non-residential building works, construction works and transport equipment, while machinery equipment spend also unexpectedly dropped. Net exports were also a drag as imports pared back the decline in 1Q and export volumes drifted lower.

## Energy available, no goods production lift

Viewed from the production side, much of the 2Q gains came from the services sector, particularly finance and business services (accounting for 0.3%pts of the GDP rise). Output of the primary and secondary sectors remains volatile, despite the absence of significant electricity constraints and improvement in logistics bottlenecks. Agricultural output eased after a strong 1Q. But mining production extended the poor 1Q performance. Manufacturing recovered much of its 1Q production losses, yet the outlook for this sector remains muted.

While we main our 1%y/y GDP forecast for this year, we view risks as being skewed to the downside. On the positive side, real wage gains are set to be a key support for growth in 2H, amplified by the cash injection made possible as part of the two-pot pension system. Early indications are of significant interest from consumers to withdraw the seed capital, up to the R30,000 limit per person. However, some potential bottlenecks are the need for a tax directive for each withdrawal. Moreover, reports so far reflect a lesser interest by higher income earners to make use of the opportunity to withdraw funds, in line with our expectations. We think full-year GDP growth is likely to be in the range of 0.8%y/y to 1%y/y.

Separately, the current account balance improved to a deficit of 0.9% in 2Q, from a downwardly revised -1.5% in 1Q. The trade surplus widened to 2.6% of GDP (from 2.3%), helped by a rise in the terms-of-trade, which dominated higher import volumes. The balance on services, income and transfers also rose to a 3.4% deficit, from -3.8%, as income payments (dividends) fell. We now project a current account deficit of 1.5% this year (from -1.7%), but this hinges on the pick up in consumer spending into year-end.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian 2Q real GDP growth printed at 0.2%q/q sa, in line with RBA and consensus forecasts

• Consumption accelerated to 0.9%ar sa in 1H24, supporting our view spending growth has troughed

Building approvals remain volatile, increasing 10%m/ m sa in July

There were few surprises in the June quarter GDP report with the economy expanding 0.2%q/q sa, while the over year ago rate slowed marginally to 1%. The prior quarter was revised one-tenth higher to 0.2%q/q sa, reflecting upgrades to private consumption earlier in the year. While the pace of spending remains far from robust, the data preserves the message that consumption bottomed in 2H23, at an average annualized pace of +0.2%, picking up to +0.9% ar sa in 1H24.

The saving rate (0.6% of disposable income, Figure 1) remains very low by historical standards and at face value remains a potential headwind to future consumption. While the ability for households to draw down on savings to facilitate consumption is clearly limited, our base line is for a modest pick up in spending in coming quarters as the combination of rising real wage growth and the implementation of tax cuts from 1<sup>st</sup> July boost incomes. Monetary policy remains restrictive and our expectation is for the cash rate to remain on hold this year, with policy easing likely from early 2025. That said, we note that a more rapid deterioration in the labor market or swifter return of inflation to target are potential catalysts for an earlier than forecast move.

Figure 1: Australian household saving rate % of disposable income
![](images/7ed05f570d8582a0eecdb59d8ca54f9875c70995743602e63683df23921b706c.jpg)

Productivity news was better this quarter, at least with respect to near-term inflationary implications, as the over year ago rate in GDP per hour worked jumped back to positive territory (+0.5%oya). That reflects a better trend for a number of quarters and its particularly strong inverse relationship to hours worked of late. Though still short of prior decade norms, these gains are a turn from the declines of late ’22-23.

## RBA standing firm

In a speech titled “ The Cost of High Inflation” RBA Governor Bullock this week reminded markets that it is still too early to declare victory over inflation, with both headline and core CPI remaining above the top of the central bank’s target band. The Governor mades it clear the outlook for policy remains data dependent, though based on the standing forecast “the Board does not expect that it will be in a position to cut rates in the near term”. The J.P. Morgan forecast is broadly aligned with the RBA view and our baseline is for inflation to gradually drift lower and return to target next year. Viewed alongside our forecast for a modest deterioration in the labor market, we regard the inflation landscape as consistent with monetary policy remaining on hold this year, with easing likely from early 2025.

## Current account: The olden days

The current account printed at A\$-8 billion in the June quarter, a return to the pre-COVID years when Australian consistently ran a current account deficit (Figure 2). The deterioration in the current account is broad based, with lower prices for Australia’s key commodity exports dragging the trade balance lower. Increased foreign ownership of Australian denominated bonds (government, banks and corporates) has delivered a surge in income debits. Income account dynamics are generally slow-moving and it’s unlikely debits return to the pre-COVID level any time soon, meaning Australia’s current account is set to deliver small deficits near-term.

Figure 2: Australian current account composition
![](images/44494227d3097633f4a76d9730ff9d21d516a754004ed378ec522b1961c0f3cc.jpg)

## Building approval bounce

Australian dwelling approvals rose 10.4%m/m in July, above both J.P. Morgan (+2%m/m sa) and consensus expectations (+2.3%m/m sa). The large rise in total approvals primarily reflects approvals for high density dwellings (+32.1%m/m sa), approvals for detached houses increased a modest 0.6%m/m sa. The rise in July more than offset the 6.4%m/m sa decline in June (Figure 3). The June report included quite a significant degree of heterogeneity both across states and between categories of approvals. Since the pandemic, this sort of cross-sectional dispersion has been associated with administrative/processing lags, which has led to large swings in approvals month to month. Some of the July strength probably represents payback for the June weakness, though the average over the past two months is about 10% higher than the average over the first 5 months of the year.

Figure 3: Australian residential building approval volumes
![](images/131b267047304f6532c519db5df4ae1fb27d4d260f7dd2dc52d63580e3858977.jpg)

## Australia

## Data releases and forecasts

Week of September 9<sup>th</sup> - 13<sup>th</sup>
<table><tr><td>Tue</td><td colspan="5">NAB business confidence</td></tr><tr><td>Sep 10</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>11:30am</td><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td></tr><tr><td>Index</td><td></td><td>1</td><td>2</td><td>1</td><td>1</td></tr></table>

We forecast stability in the August NAB business survey with the headline confidence and conditions indexes to hold at +1 and +6, respectively. Both measures are now meaningfully below the 2022/2023 highs, but remain broadly aligned with the longer run average and consistent with our view for activity to modestly improve in coming quarters. The labor market related indicators have been mixed but suggest that the jobless rate is biased higher, an unsurprising outcome given the jobless rate is still very low by historical standards and below the RBA’s estimate of NAIRU.

## Data releases and forecasts

Week of September 9<sup>th</sup> - 13<sup>th</sup>

<table><tr><td>Thur</td><td>Retail card sales</td></tr><tr><td>Sep 12</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td></tr><tr><td>8:45am</td><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>-1.2</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.1 -0.3</td></tr></table>

New Zealand’s consumption data have been strikingly weak this year, a dynamic evident in the retail card spending numbers which have contracted for six consecutive months. Drivers of this weakness are well understood with an elevated cash rate, rising unemployment and soft house price growth weighing on the consumer. Inflation has declined meaningfully in recent prints and the RBNZ has started to ease policy so it’s likely consumption starts to improve later this year. With that in mind, we think it is too early for these support factors to lift spending and forecast retail card sales declined 0.3%m/ m sa in August.

## Greater China

• China policy support re-directed and stepped up

• August PMIs mixed: NBS points to continued softness, Caixin mfg PMI picked up

• Taiwan: August mfg PMI came in lower, but stayed solid

• August CPI inflation at 2.4%oya; housing market boom still a major focus for the central bank

China’s economic growth remains soft, calling for more forceful fiscal and monetary policy actions, more balance in support between consumption and investment, and more balance between promoting service and manufacturing upgrades. We have observed some interesting policy moves in recent weeks.

On the fiscal policy front, the pace of special local government bond issuance has been lagging significantly behind the usual pace. A focus of fiscal policy implementation since May has been full utilization of existing fiscal space rather than additional fiscal stimulus. Entering August, the pace of government bond issuance has accelerated: the increase in government bonds is tracking at 1,960 billion yuan, 796 billion yuan of which are special local government bonds (cumulatively 66% of full-year 3.9 trillion yuan quota of special local government has been issued). In addition, it seems increasingly likely the government may allow for expanded use of special LGB proceeds to support consumption via trade-in or housing de-stocking measures and the like.

On the monetary policy front, the re-calibration of the Fed rate outlook has removed CNY depreciation pressure, with CNY and CNH exchange rates falling significantly below the daily fixing. After the first rate cut this year in July, we think the PBOC will follow up with further rate cuts in the coming quarters (10bp cut in 4Q and another 10bp cut in 1Q25).

On housing policy, Bloomberg reported that the Chinese government is considering allowing homeowners to refinance to lower borrowing costs for millions of families. If verified, this would be modestly positive for consumption. Allowing for mortgage refinancing is less about housing stabilization, as it mainly benefits existing mortgage borrowers but has little direct impact on new home demand. It is more importantly a measure to support consumption by increasing disposable income of beneficiary households. Our banking analysts suggest that the maximum rate cuts could be 75bps. If that applies to all outstanding mortgages (38.19 trillion yuan), annual savings from mortgage repayments could be as high as 289 billion yuan, equivalent to 0.58% of total retail sales in 2023. In practice, the impact could be much smaller.

The policy initiative to support services consumption and positive official media coverage on the launch of the new Chinese video game (Black Myth: Wukong) signal more favorable and balanced policy support for consumption and services. If this momentum sustains, we think it will help to restore market confidence and lend support to economic recovery.

## August PMIs a mixed bag

The August NBS PMI report points to continued softness in economic activity. The NBS mfg PMI fell 0.3pt to 49.1, matching the lowest reading this year (Figure 1). All key subcomponents were below the 50-threshold. Regarding service and construction sector activity, the NBS non-mfg PMI ticked up 0.1-pt to 50.3 in August, ending four consecutive months of decline. In all, the NBS composite PMI edged down 0.1pt to 50.1, the lowest reading since 2023. The NBS PMI readings suggest that overall economic activity remains soft in the near term. In particular, industry production may stay on the soft side, dragged by continued weakness in housing activity and recent slowdown in exports. Meanwhile, the Caixin mfg PMI came in modestly firmer than expected, up 0.5pt to 50.4, partly reversing the 2.0pt drop in July.

Figure 1: China manufacturing PMIs
![](images/84e9b6bca1892992eff4b9b90054c3fb1354128fa4e1fddcca4dc9af811c39a8.jpg)
Source: NBS, S&P Global, J.P. Morgan

The Caixin mfg PMI input price component fell 0.6pt to 49.9 in August, and the output price component fell 0.3pt to 49.5. For comparison, the NBS input price component dropped 6.7pts to 43.2 and the output price component fell 4.3pts to 42.0, reigniting rising concerns about the return of PPI deflation pressure. The imbalance between domestic supply and demand continues, signalling weak producer pricing power. It also points to export price deflationary pressure. For reference, export prices in USD terms fell 7.9%oya in July, or down 1.6%m/m sa on our estimate. In RMB terms, the export price fell 15.5% 3m/3m saar in July though PPI inched up 0.3%, partly due to Chinese exporters’ price competition in the global market.

## Taiwan: mfg PMI lower but still solid

Taiwan’s mfg PMI fell 1.4pts in August, following the 0.3pt fall in July. But it stayed in the expansionary zone, at 51.5 (Figure 2). Following the volatile, moderate upward trend (from subdued levels) since early 2023, Taiwan’s mfg PMI readings have picked up momentum since early this year. The latest August reading suggests the industrial sector’s constructive cyclical momentum may have continued.

Figure 2: Global and Taiwan manufacturing PMIs
![](images/55de938bffb5d4ce4d1fb74bd3d6c28a3fe1a186ada39469fe1f1c452f669043.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, J.P. Morgan

Our baseline forecasts continue to look for a steady uptrend in Taiwan’s tech exports going into 2H24. Support measures include ongoing structural strength in AI-related products and HPC-related demand, along with a gradual recovery in other tech demand areas, including PC, smartphone, and other consumer-related tech demand. The global tech sector’s de-stocking pressure is also easing. The latest guidance from major Taiwanese tech manufacturers continues to be positive on demand conditions looking ahead. And the latest signals from the August mfg PMI remain encouraging. We have also suggested that consistent upward momentum in the external sector in recent months is feeding into the domestic demand. Consumer confidence in the economic outlook has trended up further as of August. Overall, the steady upturn in employment growth (and hence household income), as well as the forceful pick-up in consumer confidence suggest solid domestic demand growth in 2H24.

## August CPI inflation at 2.36%oya

Taiwan’s headline CPI inflation came in moderately above expectations in August at 2.36%oya (Figure 3), easing modestly from 2.53%oya in July (and the recent peak of 3.59%oya in June 2022). Our seasonal adjustment process shows headline CPI rose 0.3% m/m sa in August, following the increase of 0.3% m/m sa in July, largely reflecting a nearterm typhoon-related uptick in food prices. For the first eight months of the year, headline CPI rose 2.32%oya (compared to full-year CPI inflation at 2.49% in 2023).

Looking ahead, we expect Taiwan’s headline CPI inflation to ease to modestly below 2% in September and going into

4Q, especially considering the higher base effect from late last year, with the fading of the temporary typhoon effect on food prices. We believe core CPI will similarly stay below 2%oya through the rest of the year. Overall, we expect headline CPI inflation to average 2.2% in 2024, while core CPI inflation will likely average 1.9%. On monetary policy, our baseline scenario looks for the Taiwan central bank to keep policy rates on hold through the rest of this year, with the central bank focus on property market trends.

Figure 3: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/e255c1ceefd31c1c76215cc078f83c66e5624eaededa7bdca3e320c4cbe5272f.jpg)

## Review of past week’s data

Week of September 9 - 13

The breakdown of the NBS non-mfg PMI shows that the main drags come from capital market services (anti-corruption, regulation strengthening and compensation cuts in the financial sector), housing and resident services, similar to the previous month. By contrast, summer travel season contributed to solid performance (above 55) in the railway and airline transportation, postal service, telecommunication, cultural, sports and entertainment sectors.

Purchasing managers index (31 Aug)

In the breakdown of Caixin mfg PMI, the output component of Caixin mfg PMI rose 0.5pt to 50.7, and the new order component rose 1.1pts to 50.3, both in contrast to the decline in the NBS readings. On the cautious side, the new export orders component fell 1.1pts to 49.4. The input and output price components also fell modestly in August, but much less compared to the dramatic decline in the NBS readings. Besides, the employment component inched up 0.1pt to 50.0, after staying below the 50-threshold for 11 consecutive months.

No data releases.

In the breakdown of NBS mfg PMI, the output component fell 0.3pt to 49.8, the new order component fell 0.4pt to 48.9, the new export order component rose 0.2pt to 48.7, and the employment component fell 0.2pt to 48.1. In addition, the input price component fell 6.7pts to 43.2 and the output price component fell 4.3pts to 42.0, reigniting concerns about returning PPI deflation pressure. In a breakdown by enterprise size, the mfg PMI reading for large enterprises edged down 0.1-pt to 50.4 in August, while the reading for mediumsized enterprises fell 0.7-pt to 48.7 and the reading for smallsized enterprises fell 0.3-pt to 46.4.

Regarding key components of the mfg PMI report, the output component dropped another 3.2pts to 50.7 in August, following the 1.0pt fall in July. The above 50-threshold reading points to still solid near-term momentum in industrial production activities. Regarding external demand conditions, the export orders component picked up 0.7pt to 52.9, after inching down 0.2-pt in July. It hints at a constructive nearterm outlook for external demand. Regarding supply chain conditions, the suppliers’ delivery times reading rose 3.3pts to 47.7, after the 2.9pts fall in July, suggesting shortening of suppliers’ delivery time.

<table><tr><td colspan="5">% change</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>2.4</td><td>2.5</td><td>2.2</td><td>2.4</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m sa</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td></tr></table>

Further details in the August CPI report show an uptick in food prices (reflecting near-term typhoon effect), modest easing in energy prices, and a modest rise in core CPI. In particular, food prices picked up 1.7% m/m sa in August (or up 4.6%oya), with a jump in vegetable prices (+21.7% m/m nsa) and a notable gain in fruit prices (6.7% m/m nsa), reflecting near-term typhoon effects. Besides, the aggregate energy-related CPI component fell modestly by 0.2% m/m sa in August (or up 1.2%oya).

Meanwhile, core CPI (overall CPI excluding fruit, vegetables and energy) showed rather a modest trend, rising 1.80%oya in August, compared to 1.85%oya in July, translating to a modest gain of 0.1% m/m sa (vs +0.2% m/m sa in July). In addition, general service prices rose 2.41%oya in August (vs 2.52%oya in July), or modestly up by 0.1% m/m sa (vs 0.3% m/m sa in July).

## Korea

• Customs exports and PMI turned up in August...

• … offsetting the downbeat headline from July IP

CPI inflation fell to the target in August, expecting further moderation going forward

July’s sharp contraction in IP had posed downside risk to our 3Q24 real GDP growth outlook. But customs exports and the manufacturing PMI turned up in August to offset some of the downbeat signal from IP. Meanwhile, CPI inflation fell to the 2.0% target in August from a 2.6%oya print in July, and the three-month trend gain suggests a further moderation of overyear-ago inflation for the rest of this year. Stabilizing inflation should provide a rationale for the Bank of Korea to ease, to be balanced against concerns over the re-acceleration of household credit growth.

## Customs exports turned up in August

Customs exports rose 3.5%m/m, sa in August after falling 1.7% in July, as hinted by our earlier estimates from the flash 20-day data (+4.4%m/m, sa). As a result, the three-month trend growth turned up to 11.2%3m/3m, saar (Figure 1). 3Q nominal export growth is likely to be slower than the threemonth growth in August and the strong 2Q growth (13.1% ar), yet the customs trade data suggest the robust underlying trend in growth continued through mid-year.

Figure 1: Korea customs exports
![](images/04f3625fdb337eecf9484d4ab7bf7863f04291c6c2ef2d67f1dee088d92365e1.jpg)

In the product details, both tech and non-tech exports bounced in August after July and recent months’ weakness. The tech upswing continues with strength in nominal exports growth (mostly led by price effect in recent months), while the weak automobile production in July is likely to be temporary.

## Manufacturing PMI firmed in August

The manufacturing PMI was up 0.5pt to 51.9 in August, after a 0.6pt fall in July. Since May, the PMI has risen by 2.5pt cumulatively. The details were positive, with an upturn in the output index and a rise in the new order index, amid a hint of easing in supply-side bottlenecks. Notably, the output index rebounded 1.2pt to 53.7 which is the highest level since April 2021. The current level of output PMI suggests an upside risk to three-month trend growth in IP by July (Figure 2). That is consistent with our reading after the weak July IP data that output should recover in the later months of 3Q. The new order index recovered 0.7pt to 51.8 after falling 2.2pt in July, while the new exports order index fell 1.8pt to 50.4. While the order indices remain at robust levels, their recent moderation suggests caution on the medium-term outlook.

Figure 2: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/542a9d2bfb0505aab2e6552b7831d031d5cc449ab9731a8f14c12898b7c9a967.jpg)

## CPI inflation fell to the target

The consumer price index rose 2.0%oya in August, meeting the 2% target after a 2.6%oya print in July. August’s CPI inflation was long expected to record a soft %oya print given the high base in 2023 August. In line with recent months, the monthly gain was modest to fulfill such expectations.

Figure 3: Inflation forecast
![](images/be674c927901f97b21538811bf670a3a073cf3b1f1ed1ff20b48d83a9707feee.jpg)
Seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, the headline CPI rose 1.0% 3m/3m, saar, and the core CPI gain was also modest at 1.6%3m/3m, saar in trend. We expect that %oya inflation will fall below the 2% target from September (Figure 3), given the core price trend and mixed readings in the supply-side price pressures.
