Figure 1: Indonesia consumer prices %3m/3m, saar

## ASEAN

• Indonesia’s disinflation is driven by falling food prices

• Despite weaker rice tariff cut pass-through, Philippines CPI remains firmly within target

• Stable food and energy prices keep Thailand CPI on track towards returning to lower bound of target

The August inflation prints in ASEAN were in line with expectations and continue to support the case for central bank easing. Thanks to significant food disinflation, Indonesia’s CPI could slip below target by end of the year. Despite the weaker and delayed pass-through from rice tariff cuts, Philippines inflation remains firmly within target. Thailand’s CPI is also set to return to the lower bound of the 1%-3% target by year-end.

## Indonesia: CPI dodges weather disruptions

Headline and core inflation in Indonesia in August remained unchanged at 2.1%oya and 2.0% respectively, generally in line with expectations. The latest print adds to the significant easing in price momentum that we have seen since 2Q24, especially in headline inflation (Figure 1).

![](images/4837abe30ceaca67565a268d7322b36983426fbea41fbdb2da2cde6420feab50.jpg)
Source: BPS, J.P. Morgan

Similar to prior months, food disinflation, and particularly volatile raw food items (e.g., shallot, poultry) was the major contributor to softer overall prices (-0.15%pt. contribution to the monthly headline change; Figure 2). We think this reflects the ongoing dividends of supply-side reforms undertaken in recent years (e.g., improve transportation networks, establish real-time price monitoring systems, establish National Food Agency) that have made the domestic agriculture market more resilient to supply-side shocks. Another driver of disinflation in August was lower transport prices (0.1% m/m, sa; 1.3% oya), consistent with softening global crude oil prices.

Figure 2: Indonesia: sources of inflation
![](images/5654505f692b2f15d052d55f6257848821ff25d8d5dcdee1d00875dc1c8839fe.jpg)

Given recent FX stability, ongoing fiscal support (in the form of energy subsidies), and favorable base effects, we anticipate headline CPI to drop below 1%, which is below the 1.5%-3.5% target range by the second quarter of 2025. We expect it to move toward 2.5% by the end of 2025 (Figure 3). This provides the degrees of freedom for Bank Indonesia (BI) to ease monetary policy. We continue to expect BI to lower the policy rate by 100bps, from 6.25% to 5.25% by mid-2025.

Figure 3: Indonesia headline CPI
![](images/e37b64a2c114935b253651276a31b7b03cc24e91f5280009c4a7792edf826beb.jpg)

## Philippines: Tariff effect takes time

Headline inflation rose 3.3%oya in August (July: 4.4%oya), higher than the JPM estimate (2.5%oya) but lower than consensus expectations (3.6%oya). This is the lowest CPI reading since January 2024 following the above-target print in July. Core inflation also softened from 2.8%oya in July to 2.6%oya in August. On a sequential basis, headline and core CPI fell 0.1%m/m, sa and rose 0.1%m/m, sa, respectively, with the underlying trend continuing to downshift (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Philippines headline and core inflation %3m/3m, saar
![](images/da06d6103d49a467fff02e4067c581573f544978c5b04613aa86dc34caadcdc6.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

The primary catalyst of last month's disinflation was the drop in transport fuel prices (-1.6% m/m, sa; -0.2% oya), consistent with the recent decline in global crude oil prices. If global growth concerns continue to exert downward pressure on energy prices, we anticipate that pump prices could decrease further.

However, the key observation in last month’s CPI print is the underwhelming drop in retail rice prices (-1% on average) since the rice tariff reduction (from 35% to 15%) in early-July. In our view, private importers and traders have been reluctant to lower prices on rice stocks bought at higher tariffs, leading to increased inventory levels (Figure 5). Depending on storage methods and shelf life (estimates vary from one to two years), these stocks may remain off the market for a while, weakening the pass-through of tariff cuts to retail prices. Nonetheless, we continue to see the broader trend of rice disinflation as terms-of-trade improve.

Figure 5: Philippines rice stock inventory
![](images/15425e65646a8536938512b13f42164c672cd0b8c33c6b3b0227249517ae70ef.jpg)

Following the August CPI print, we revise our 2024 CPI forecast slightly higher (from 3.1% to 3.3%) to incorporate the slower pass-through of the rice tariff cut but the sequential momentum is tracking 2.5%oya, which is firmly within the target range. Coupled with FX stability, we continue to expect the BSP to deliver another 200bp of rate cuts through end-2025 (Figure 6), with the risk skewed towards a more frontloaded policy normalization path.

Figure 6: Philippines real policy rate
![](images/420240a343f2c21fffa1703e5c1e6014dfbff3ca27e724896b8ca7a345b3ee62.jpg)

## Thailand: Tracking back to target

Headline and core inflation settled at 0.4%oya and 0.6%oya respectively in August, broadly in line with expectations. The underlying trend for both gauges remain soft, although core CPI is picking up very gradually. Monthly price gains (0.1%m/m, sa) was driven by higher raw food (0.18%pt. contribution) and core (0.12%pt.) prices, with a significant offset by weaker energy prices (-0.23%pt.).

The decline in energy CPI was driven by unsubsidized transport fuel prices, in-line with the recent drop in global crude oil prices. However, we find that the diesel price cap (THB33 per liter) slowed the pace of energy disinflation, given that the free-floating diesel price is estimated to be below the administered price in the past two months (Figure 7). As a result, for the first time since December 2023, the Oil Fund has imposed a net positive levy on diesel to maintain the price cap amid falling ex-refinery costs.

Figure 7: Thailand diesel price
![](images/fd8a66a0781d921ba6c9afadca65a334af3f4a6519785e34fabe2765642f7dd9.jpg)

The uptick in raw food prices (0.18%pt.) was concentrated in vegetables (0.16%pt.) likely affected by flood-related disrup tions. However, with overall agricultural output still unaffected and in the absence of a broad-based increase in input costs (e.g., electricity, logistics, labor), we think that the passthrough of higher food PPI to CPI will continue to be limited (Figure 8).

Figure 8: Thailand agriculture PPI vs. food CPI
![](images/082b919096383a41fbf35144a078a1b7991fd791470760516516bef6eb277ac5.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Following the August print, we continue to expect headline CPI to rise gradually towards the lower bound of the 1%-3% target range by early-4Q24 and to peak at around 1.8%oya by early-1Q25 (Figure 9). This benign CPI profile supports our view that the Bank of Thailand (BoT) will recalibrate the restrictiveness of their monetary policy by delivering two 25bp rate cuts in 4Q24 (likely December) and 1Q25.

Figure 9: Thailand headline vs. core CPI
![](images/101c5f1a70ea1e06b11278925bba9d33f29dbe426a08edc89987f7e7dfc35817.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan forecast in shaded area

## India

August CPI inflation expected to ease to 3.3% oya, helped by base effects

• Food prices likely fell in the month; core likely to remain benign

• July IP report to provide a gauge of activity following elections and heat wave

• Corporate profitability dips in 2Q

The main datapoint next week is CPI inflation. We expect 3.3% oya in August – easing from 3.5% in July – though both the July and August prints have been helped by large and favorable base effects (Figure 1). Notwithstanding that, headline CPI prices are expected to fall 0.3% m/m, sa, thanks to declining food prices. Within the food basket, there appears to be a broad-based decline of food prices in August across cereals, pulses, oilseeds and most vegetable prices, barring onions (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/3575887c8263880bb1c27113f30e61ea04bf3bfc377d0e66fde8ab8bc7aebc13.jpg)

Figure 2: Food ex vegetables
![](images/67ced970f3afb74929c311e6348d6c8ea09f0ced7e1fa909a6274729a394949d.jpg)

Core-core prices rose 0.4% m/m, sa in July owing to a (presumably one-off) increase in telecom tariffs. Net of the tariff hike, core-core prices remained benign, increasing 0.3% m/m, sa in July, which has been the average for most of 2024. Core prices are again expected to rise 0.3-0.4% m/m, sa in August, conditional on whether some of the telecom tariff change spilled into August. On an over-year-ago basis, this would translate into core-core inflation in the 3.4-3.5% range in August.

With core inflation benign for a while, RBI concerns are focused around food inflation. With the monsoon tracking 8% above normal, healthy sowing and softer global food prices, the outlook for food inflation is looking progressively more constructive. If our August forecast is realized, then 3Q24 average CPI inflation is tracking 3.8%, which is 60 bps below the RBI’s forecast of 4.4%.

## July IP preview

July IP will be an important gauge of the activity after the heat wave and elections. Softness in parts of activity in 2Q24 was attributed to both these factors (Figure 3). Manufacturing production averaged just 0.1% m/m, sa in 2Q24 vs. 0.6% in 1Q24. On the other hand, the heat wave also induced a jump in coal and power production to meet surging power demand. Consequently, both mining and power production are likely to decline in July. All told, we expect IP to contract 0.1% m/m, sa in July, which would translate into a 4.6% oya print.

Figure 3: Manufacturing IP
![](images/9c4dce95ad71be8fb2ce263ae4f91ffc7ff862594d50369eb2d63fc335a189b7.jpg)

## Growth in corporate profits eases in 2Q

Growth in nominal corporate profits eased further in 2Q24, slowing to 11.3% oya in 2Q24 from 12.6% in 1Q24 and 20.2% in 4Q23. Profits were boosted in 3Q and 4Q last year by a sharp fall in commodity prices. As these commodity price adjustments fade, profit growth has been normalizing. Should the recent sharp drop of oil prices persist and spill over to other industrial commodities, profits could see another boost. Manufacturing firm profit growth also softened to 10% oya in 1Q24 and 2Q24 from 19% and 27% in 4Q23 and 3Q23, respectively, due to lower commodity prices. In the coming quarters, if there is no further shock, profit growth could be expected to converge to top-line (sales) growth, which has remained in the 7-9% range in the last year.

Overall sales growth was 8.6% oya in 2Q24, inching down from 9.1% oya in 1Q24. Similarly, manufacturing sales growth eased to 6.1% oya in 2Q24 from 7.8% in 1Q24 (Figure 4). Information technology sales also remained weak, printing at just 2.5% oya in 2Q24, not far from 1.4% in 1Q24. Only the services sector (ex IT firms) reflects strong top-line (28% oya) and bottom-line (16% oya) growth. All this suggests that, barring a commodity price shock, growth in corporate profits is likely to slow further in the coming quarters.

Figure 4: Manufacturing Companies Sales and Profits
![](images/2f55d62d829513d4239566804497cadf0cee19c3487c61f4fffeebfbb6d31b7e.jpg)

Listed staff expenses growth, a good proxy for formal sector jobs, has remained on a downtrend, reflecting a slowing in formal jobs markets. The weakness is most acute in IT firms, where staff costs are now falling. This doesn’t augur well for urban consumption demand, which already has been ebbing in the last couple of quarters (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Staff costs of private listed corporates % oya
![](images/2b6978744c8339ffc22b36a548bf264e4a2f458c646b75db76d435c3c6f6c6ae.jpg)

Note that these data on corporate performance, apart from providing a signal of the state of economy, are used extensively in GDP computation. That is particularly the case in the manufacturing sector, so they have a large imprint on reported GDP growth.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
